# Spec: P1 foundation and money (the JewelVault backend: Node.js API and MariaDB)

Draft 5.1 follows draft 5 the same evening and has three jobs, all listed in 18.12: Harsh decided not to use Cloudflare R2, so **backups stay on his Hostinger plan** (a backup folder next to the site plus Hostinger's own daily backup) and the owner's monthly **Download everything** becomes the one copy outside Hostinger, with a reminder, a health flag and a test (B2, B3, I12, O45, O53); the Hostinger proof ran (verdict GO) and its measured rules are written in (S8, S11, A12, H7, I8, O38, O48 and a new row 20 of section 0); and the domain must be Siddhi's own (O38). Draft 4 moved the spec from Supabase and Postgres to the stack Harsh chose on 15 October 2026: the frontend stays on Vercel (O39), the backend is a **Node.js API on his Hostinger Business plan**, and the database is **MariaDB 11.8** on the same plan. Harsh approved the spec change (decision D9 of `NODE-DESIGN.md`, DEC-9 in its reviewed version) and decided that photos stay in MariaDB behind a `PhotoStore` interface. Not legal or tax advice. Anything legal is flagged `[CA]` for Siddhi's CA and is not decided here.

## How to read this spec

- Every requirement is one testable sentence with an ID (`D3`, `X5`), a **Test** (IDs `T-A01` ... in section 15) and a **Source** (QA finding, hazard, review finding or document).
- **"As coded"** means the behaviour of the prototype in `Client/design/jewelvault/jewelvault/src`, kept on purpose (Harsh's ruling: freeze the rules, fix only proven bugs). The file and line are cited so the reviewer can check.
- `[NEEDS CLARIFICATION: question, who answers, default]` marks something research cannot settle. Each has a default so the build is never blocked. **Every one is collected in section 16.**
- Money is exact. No floating-point number is used for money or weight anywhere in the database or the API, and money and weight cross the wire as decimal strings, never as JSON numbers (S3).
- Section 18 lists, for each review finding, where draft 2 resolves it; 18.10 lists every change of draft 4, section by section, with the reason (history); **18.11 lists every change of draft 5 against the review finding that caused it; 18.12 lists every change of draft 5.1 against the instruction or proof result that caused it, so that a third reviewer can check the changed lines only.**
- Evidence marks in section 0 and in the sources of Part A: **[V]** read in the official documentation on 15 Oct 2026 by the writer of draft 4; **[S]** measured by the database spike on a local MariaDB 11.8.9 (not on Hostinger); **[W]** measured by the writer of draft 4 or draft 5 on 15 Oct 2026 on a throw-away local MariaDB 11.8.9 (a container with no volume, removed afterwards; not on Hostinger); **[H]** read from Harsh's own Hostinger account; **[P]** measured on Harsh's real Hostinger plan by the API-00 proof apps (the writer did not re-run it; the reports are `HOSTINGER-SPIKE.md`, `API-00-RESULTS-RUNTIME.md` and `API-00-RESULTS-DB.md` in `jewelvault-api-design`, and `NODE-DESIGN.md` 7.5 and 7.6); **[N]** a claim of `NODE-DESIGN.md` that was not re-read for draft 4 or 5; **[U]** unverified. Where a rule rests on [N] or [U] it says so and names the open item (O48) that tests it.

## 0. Research pass (14 October 2026; redone for the new stack on 15 October 2026)

Sources read for this spec. The marks [V], [S], [W], [H], [P], [N] and [U] are explained in "How to read this spec". Row texts were fetched on 15 Oct 2026 through the MariaDB documentation repository (the text of mariadb.com/kb) and the Node.js 24 and mysql2 documentation in Context7, and by fetching Vercel's pages. Rows 17 to 19 are unchanged from draft 3 (read on 14 October).

| # | Source | What it confirms | Used in |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | MariaDB docs: `innodb_lock_wait_timeout`, `innodb_rollback_on_timeout`, errors 1205, 1213 and 1020, `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE WAIT n` / `NOWAIT` | A lock wait timeout (error 1205) rolls back **the statement, not the transaction**; the whole transaction only if `innodb_rollback_on_timeout` is on, which is global, not dynamic and off by default (so Hostinger will not change it). `innodb_lock_wait_timeout` has session scope, is dynamic, defaults to 50 and ranges 0 to 100,000,000 seconds. Error 1020 ("Record has changed since last read") rolls back the **whole** transaction. A deadlock is detected at once and one transaction is rolled back (1213). `FOR UPDATE WAIT n` and `NOWAIT` exist. [V] The 1205 behaviour was measured too, and so was 1020 under REPEATABLE READ. [S] | S8, S11, S12, X0 |
| 2 | MariaDB docs: `max_statement_time`, `SET STATEMENT ... FOR`, error 1969 | A statement running longer than `max_statement_time` ends with error 1969 (`ER_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT`, SQLSTATE 70100); `SET STATEMENT var=value FOR statement` sets it for one statement. [V] That it is a session variable a normal user may set, and that it also cuts a lock wait, was measured. [S] It limits one statement, not a transaction. [S] The pages fetched did not say whether fractional seconds are allowed; they are: `SET SESSION max_statement_time = 0.5` was accepted and a `SLEEP(2)` was cut with error 1969. [W] | S11, S12, C4 |
| 3 | MariaDB docs: statements that cause an implicit commit; the privilege `TRUNCATE` needs | `CREATE TABLE`, `ALTER TABLE`, `CREATE INDEX`, `CREATE TRIGGER`, `DROP ...`, `TRUNCATE TABLE`, `GRANT` and `START TRANSACTION` commit the open transaction at once, so **DDL cannot be rolled back**. `TRUNCATE TABLE` needs the DROP privilege, not DELETE. [V] With Delete and Drop removed from a user, `DELETE`, `DROP TABLE` and `TRUNCATE` were refused (error 1142) and ordinary reads and writes worked. [S] On 11.8.9 with the hPanel permission list minus Delete and Drop, `DELETE` gave error 1142, `TRUNCATE` gave "DROP command denied" (1142), and `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`, `SHOW GRANTS` and an `UPDATE` that empties a `MEDIUMBLOB` all worked. [W] | S7, S9, T-B18 |
| 4 | MariaDB docs: `sql_mode` | The default is `STRICT_TRANS_TABLES, ERROR_FOR_DIVISION_BY_ZERO, NO_AUTO_CREATE_USER, NO_ENGINE_SUBSTITUTION`; an out-of-range value is error 1264 in strict mode and a **warning with a clamped or zero value** otherwise. [V] Hostinger's server runs `NO_AUTO_CREATE_USER,NO_ENGINE_SUBSTITUTION`, which is not strict. [H] Reproduced locally: an over-range amount became 999999999999.99, `'abc'` became 0 and a long text was cut, all without an error. [S] | S4, S11 |
| 5 | MariaDB docs: isolation levels, `START TRANSACTION WITH CONSISTENT SNAPSHOT` | At READ COMMITTED every consistent read takes its own fresh snapshot; at REPEATABLE READ one read view serves the whole transaction and `WITH CONSISTENT SNAPSHOT` creates it at once; at READ COMMITTED it affects only the first read. At READ COMMITTED InnoDB takes no gap locks for locking reads, UPDATE and DELETE, **except** the duplicate-key check on a unique index, which takes a next-key lock at every level; foreign-key checks take no gap locks there. [V] Hostinger's isolation level is READ-COMMITTED. [H] The nightly check needs REPEATABLE READ set first, and `FOR UPDATE` on a row that does not exist does not block its insertion at READ-COMMITTED. [S] | S11, X0, I8 |
| 6 | MariaDB docs: CHECK constraints, `information_schema.CHECK_CONSTRAINTS` | A failing CHECK raises error 4022 (4025 was seen for column checks [S]); `CHECK_CONSTRAINTS` lists the constraint name, table and `CHECK_CLAUSE` text, so the clause can be read and compared with a constant. [V] A CHECK that evaluates to NULL passes; a subquery (error 1970) and `CURDATE()` (error 1901) are refused; `ALTER TABLE ... DROP CONSTRAINT` and `ADD CONSTRAINT` work. [S] `CHECK_CLAUSE` comes back normalised, for example `` `grand_total` >= 0 and `grand_total` <= 1000000000.00 ``, so comparing a cap with the constants file is a small parse. [W] Not run: reading every clause of our own schema (T-F02 does it). | S4, D8, T-F02 |
| 7 | MariaDB docs: `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`, `LAST_INSERT_ID(expr)` | Affected rows are 1 for an insert and 2 for an update, unless the client sets `CLIENT_FOUND_ROWS`, in which case an unchanged duplicate also reports 1; `LAST_INSERT_ID(expr)` makes the next `LAST_INSERT_ID()` return expr. [V] With mysql2's default flags a duplicate reports 1 like a fresh insert, so repeat-safety uses a claim token; the counter pattern gave numbers 1 to N with no gap under rollback, kill and timeout; a duplicate insert waits for an in-flight twin. [S] | L3, L5 |
| 8 | MariaDB docs and spike: column types | `ROUND` on fixed-point types rounds half up. `MEDIUMBLOB` holds up to 16,777,215 bytes. `JSON` is an alias of `LONGTEXT`. [V] `JSON` is `LONGTEXT` plus a `JSON_VALID` CHECK; the native `UUID` type validates its text, takes 16 bytes and cannot be a foreign key to `CHAR(36)`; a `PERSISTENT` generated column can carry a UNIQUE (one walk-in customer); `DATETIME(6)` takes `ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)`, `NOW()` is fixed per statement and `SYSDATE()` is the real clock; a regular-expression CHECK is case-insensitive on the default collation, so the PAN rule needs a binary comparison; `key`, `lines`, `rows`, `order`, `release`, `usage`, `range`, `read` and `write` fail unquoted. [S] The default collation of a fresh 11.8.9 server is `utf8mb4_uca1400_ai_ci`, not `utf8mb4_unicode_ci` (Hostinger's server default is the latter [H]); a composite foreign key `(bill_id, customer_id)` is refused with error 1005 (errno 150) until the parent has an index that starts with (`id`, `customer_id`) and works with `UNIQUE (id, customer_id)`; an `UPDATE` that names `updated_at` keeps the value given, and one that does not name it moves it through `ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)`; 1.239 in a `DECIMAL(14,2)` is stored as 1.24 in strict mode without an error. [W] | S2, S3, D3, D6, D10, P1 |
| 9 | mysql2 (the Node driver) documentation | `DECIMAL` and `NEWDECIMAL` come back as strings unless `decimalNumbers` is on; `JSON` comes back already parsed unless `jsonStrings` is on; `supportBigNumbers` and `dateStrings` exist. [V] A JavaScript object passed as a parameter to `query()` is expanded into `col = val` pairs, which is a trap. [S] | S3, S12 |
| 10 | Node.js 24 API documentation | `crypto.argon2` (Argon2id; `memory` in KiB, `passes`, `parallelism`, a `nonce` of at least 8 bytes) and `crypto.scrypt` (default cost 16384, block size 8, parallelization 1, `maxmem` default 32 MiB) are in the v24 API. ## 1. Scope, roles and conventions

### 1.1 Scope

- **In scope (milestone M1, billing live):** login with roles (own accounts and server-side sessions in the API); stock (designs and pieces, owner-only weight); customers; bills (read-only; correction by re-issue); payments, silver-fine settlements, refunds and void; derived balances and the ledger view; opening balances and the import hooks; daily rates and the stale-rate warning; photos (stored in the database behind a `PhotoStore` interface); audit log; backups and restore (on the hosting plan) and the owner's monthly Download everything; nightly checks; the API and the cache contract between the screens and the API. **The refund function (X11) is the first thing to leave M1 if the calendar slips** (until then a same-day mistake is a void).
- **Forward-compatible, not built in M1:** edit a bill without losing payments, Order Book, public catalogue, stock search, tags, Excel upload, new document types, partial returns. The schema in Part A leaves room for them (section 17) so they need no breaking migration.
- **Out of scope for this spec:** tax invoices and bills of supply (decision 4), partial returns and credit notes, old-gold purchase, karigar ledger, Hindi interface, privacy-law screens (DPDP, due about 13 May 2027, 18 months after the Rules of 13 November 2025 as QA T7 reads them; a shorter period was proposed but none is notified; a secondary-source reading `[CA]`, and what that law asks for erasure and retention is in O34), e-way bills, the in-app staff screen, the printed layout (task P3-T1).

### 1.2 Words

- **Bill** is what the prototype calls an "estimate" or "invoice" (`Invoice` in `mockData.ts`). It is not a GST tax invoice. `[CA]`
- **Piece** (SKU) is one physical item with a unique code. **Design** groups pieces that look alike.
- **NW** is `customerNetWeight`, the weight used for billing. **Original net weight** is the hidden measured weight (`originalNetWeight`).
- **Fine** is the silver weight owed on a wholesale silver bill, settled later in silver or in cash at that day's silver rate.
- **Piroi** is an amount added per line. As coded it is added as rupees (`BillingPOSClient.tsx:54`). Whether it is rupees or grams is open (O1).
- **Receipt** is money received, silver received, or money handed back (a refund).
- **Advance** is money (or silver) a customer has paid that is not applied to any bill; it is derived, never stored (V2).
- **Re-issue** is cancelling a bill and issuing a corrected one in one step, keeping the money received (X10).
- **Opening balance** is what a customer owed (or had paid in advance) on the day the shop moves to this system (D15).
- **Owner, Biller, Staff, anon** are the four kinds of caller (1.3).
- **API** is the Node.js program on Hostinger that is the only door to the database; the browser never reaches MariaDB. **Service function** is one of the twelve money and stock functions X1 to X12 (section 10): one API call, one database connection, one transaction. **Idempotency-Key** is the request header that carries the repeat-safety id (L5); it is what draft 3 called `p_request_id`.

### 1.3 Roles

| Role | Who | May do | May not do |
|---|---|---|---|
| **owner** | Siddhi (the old `Admin`) | Everything below, plus read and write the owner-only weight, cancel and re-issue bills, void receipts, refund, add opening balances, set settings, archive, read the audit log and the nightly check, change another user's role or `active` flag (the screen for it is M2; in M1 Harsh does it through the admin door, U3) | Delete anything (nobody can); change her own role |
| **biller** | The person at the billing counter (new) | Read stock and customers; create customers and edit their details; create bills; take payments; settle fine; add today's rate; read bills, receipts and the ledger | Read the owner-only weight; cancel, re-issue, void or refund (default, O3); change stock, settings or roles; open reports and settings |
| **staff** | Inventory staff (the old `Staff`) | Read and create stock; edit stock fields; set non-sale statuses; archive stock; add categories; print tags | Read the owner-only weight; see customers, bills, receipts or the ledger; bill; set rates |
| **anon** | A caller with no session (anyone with the public site address) | Nothing in M1 except signing in, the health check and fetching a photo whose id it knows (P3). In M2 only the public-catalogue rows | Everything else |

R-role-1. The role of a signed-in user comes from the `users` table, read on every request, and nowhere else (A2). *Source:* SEC-02.

**Screens by role (a front-end rule that mirrors the API's rules; the API is the real wall):**

| Screen (route) | owner | biller | staff |
|---|---|---|---|
| `/` dashboard (rate card, KPIs, charts, activity, nightly-check line, the owner's Download everything card) | all | quick actions and KPIs without charts | quick actions, stock KPIs |
| `/billing-pos`, `/estimate`, `/customer-ledger` | yes | yes (read-only bills) | no |
| `/inventory-management`, `/design-detail`, `/print-tags` | yes | read only | yes |
| `/add-design`, `/edit-stock` | yes | no | yes |
| `/reports` (date controls and export buttons hidden in M1, O32), `/settings` | yes | no | no |
| `/scan-detail` (scan a tag; the phone menu is M2, so M1 keeps the prototype's owner-only access) | yes | no | no |
| `/order-book`, `/catalogue`, `/customer-catalogue` | hidden in M1 | hidden | hidden |

The Estimate screen's date editor and edit mode (`EstimateViewClient.tsx:113-159`) are removed in M1 (K1, Y8), and the draft screens no longer show a "next number" before saving (L4, section 18, AC6).

### 1.4 Conventions (apply to every table and function)

- **S1.** One door. The browser talks only to the API (a Node.js program on Hostinger); the database is never reachable from a browser, and the only program that talks to it in daily use is the API, with a credential kept in Hostinger's environment variables and in Harsh's password manager (U10, A3). During a migration or an import window (O49, IM1) the credential is also on Harsh's Mac and Remote MySQL is on for his address only; the window ends by switching Remote MySQL off again, a step of the go or no-go checklist (B5). phpMyAdmin is a second door for the database owner (A1). There are no exposed schemas, no generic table routes and no GraphQL (A11). Internal tables that no screen reads directly carry the prefix `sys_` (`sys_counters`, `sys_idempotency`, `sys_import_batches`) so that they cannot be confused with business tables; the other tables that no screen reads directly are `sessions`, `sys_login_attempts`, `sys_job_runs`, `sys_photo_copies` and `sys_error_log` (D24, D25); `users` (D1) is read only through its allow-list (A4). *Test:* T-D01, T-D16, T-D17. *Source:* PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md section 0; NODE-DESIGN section 0.
- **S2.** Identifiers are native `UUID` (MariaDB 11.8 type, 16 bytes), made by `crypto.randomUUID()` in the API (version 4), never a clock or a count. There is **one** id type everywhere: a foreign key from `UUID` to `CHAR(36)` is refused [S]. A design's id may be generated by the screen before `create_design` runs (X8, P2), so that a retry or a photo upload can name it; every other id and every human number is made by the API (L3). *Test:* T-C01, T-C03. *Source:* hazard 4, BIL-01, INV-09; docs #8.
- **S3.** Money is `DECIMAL(14,2)` (rupees and paise). Weight is `DECIMAL(12,3)` (grams to the milligram). A rate per gram is `DECIMAL(12,2)`. A count is `integer`. No `FLOAT`, `DOUBLE` or `REAL` column or expression exists in any money path. **No money or weight crosses the wire as a JSON number**: requests and responses carry them as decimal strings (`"257612.00"`, `"35.420"`; responses give money with 2 decimals and weight with 3), because a number cannot carry a trailing zero (S4) and loses digits above 2^53; a JSON number in a money or weight field is refused with `JV_INVALID_INPUT`, and so is a text that does not match the pattern (`^\d+(\.\d{1,2})?$` for money, up to three decimals for weight: no sign, no spaces, no `.5` and no `5.`). Both refusals are made by the Zod layer **before** `pricing.ts` sees the value, because `parseRupees` and `parseGrams` of the shared file accept numbers and loose text on purpose (the screen passes them) and the file must stay byte-identical (C12, T-A14). The API computes with integers (paise, milligrams) and writes DECIMAL text; mysql2 returns `DECIMAL` as text (docs #9). DOUBLE gave the wrong rupee on 9,987 of 20,000 deliberate half-way cases where DECIMAL gave none [S]. **A money or weight value bound as a text parameter is never used in SQL arithmetic, `ROUND` or a comparison**: MariaDB turns the text into a `DOUBLE` there, so `ROUND('2.50', 0)` is 2 where `ROUND(CAST('2.50' AS DECIMAL(14,2)), 0)` is 3 [W]. The money path does its sums in Node on integers (S12 (6)); a statement that must compute in SQL casts first (`CAST(? AS DECIMAL(14,2))`), and a source check finds any other use (T-D11). *Test:* T-A11, T-A09, T-F02, T-F09, T-D11. *Source:* hazard 5; docs #8, #9.
- **S4.** Sanity caps are enforced by the API's parsers and again by named `CHECK` constraints, **on inputs and on computed amounts**: a weight is at most 100,000 g; a rate or a piece price is at most 100,000,000; a quantity is 1 to 1,000; a bill has 1 to 200 lines; every line amount (metal amount, labour amount, piroi and other charges), line total, subtotal and grand total, and every payment, refund, discount, additional charge, opening balance and `fine_cash` amount (grams × rate), is at most 1,000,000,000. A value over a cap is answered with `JV_INVALID_INPUT` by the API, never with a database error (a `CHECK` that fires is error 4025 and is treated as a bug, S8). An input with more decimals than its column (more than 2 on a rate or an amount, more than 3 on a weight) is refused with `JV_INVALID_INPUT` too, **by the API alone**: MariaDB rounds `1.239` to `1.24` without any error and a `CHECK` sees only the rounded value [S], so it would otherwise be priced unrounded and stored rounded; the screens round before sending. A trailing zero counts, as the value was sent (`scale()` of the number): `1.230` is refused as a rate and accepted as a weight, and the screens never send one. Negative values are rejected everywhere (except `round_off`, D11). The caps are written as literals in the `CHECK` constraints and also in the API's constants file (`limits`, the only place the code reads them from); test T-F02 reads every `CHECK` from `information_schema.CHECK_CONSTRAINTS` and compares it with the constants file, so the two cannot drift. A `CHECK`, a default, a generated column and a view use built-in functions only, and a `CHECK` cannot contain a subquery or `CURDATE()` (errors 1970 and 1901 [S]), so a rule such as "a date is not in the future" lives in the API; a `CHECK` that evaluates to NULL passes [S], so every column a `CHECK` uses is `NOT NULL`. Every connection sets a strict `sql_mode` (S11), because Hostinger's is not strict and a non-strict session clamps an over-range amount to the column maximum without any error [H][S]. *Test:* T-A09, T-A16, T-A18, T-F02, T-F08. *Source:* BIL-04, INV-03, DAT-10, F19, RF-4, RM-11, RM-12; docs #4, #6.
- **S5.** The business time zone is `Asia/Kolkata`. A bill or receipt carries a calendar `date` (the business day) and a timestamp (when it was saved). Timestamps are `DATETIME(6)` in UTC (every connection sets `time_zone = '+00:00'`, S11). "Today" is computed in the API, in the `Asia/Kolkata` zone, from the API's one clock (never from the server's own time zone, which Hostinger reports as UTC [H] but the code does not rely on), and every function takes the time from that clock, so a test can fix it by injecting a fake clock; the seam is unreachable from any route (the production build offers no way to set it). The clock never returns the same instant twice: a second reading in the same microsecond gets one microsecond more, so two rows made in one transaction never tie and the ledger order of V3 and the stale check of A5 keep working (draft 3 had `clock_timestamp()`, which also advances between statements). Reports group by the `date` columns, never by a UTC timestamp: a void and a cancel carry their own calendar date (`voided_on`, `cancelled_on`, D11, D13), taken from this clock like `paid_on`, so no query ever converts a UTC stamp into a day (a void made at 00:10 Kolkata time on 1 January is stamped 18:40 UTC on 31 December and must land on 1 January, T-B16). The clock rule is a unit test (T-F09): a thousand readings inside one microsecond are strictly increasing, also from a fake clock fixed at one instant. *Test:* T-B16, T-F07, T-F09. *Source:* LED-08, DAT-11, RM-15; docs #8.
- **S6.** Every table has `created_at`, `updated_at` and, where a person acts, `created_by`, except the three append-only logs `audit_log`, `check_runs` and `backup_runs`, which have the one time column that D16 and D17 give them (`at`, `ran_at`), and the internal tables of D24 and D25, which have the time columns named there. `created_at` and `updated_at` are `DATETIME(6)` columns with `DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)` and `ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP(6)`; the API sets **both explicitly** from its clock (S5) on every insert and update it makes, so a test that fakes the clock fakes every stamp of a row, not only its dates, and the column defaults are a backstop: a change typed by hand in phpMyAdmin also moves `updated_at`, which the polling needs (C4), **but only when the statement does not name that column** [W]: an update that names it with its old value (a row editor may send every column) keeps the old stamp and the polling never sees the change, and so does an update that changes nothing. The runbook (B5) therefore fixes a row only in the SQL tab with an `UPDATE` that leaves `updated_at` out, and ends any bulk fix with `UPDATE ... SET updated_at = NOW(6)` and a bump of `data_epoch` (C4). `NOW(6)` is fixed per statement and `SYSDATE(6)` is the real clock [S]. The columns that name a person (`created_by`, `updated_by`, `set_by`, `confirmed_by`, `voided_by`, `cancelled_by`) are **always set by the API from the session user**; a value sent by a client is refused as an unknown field (A5) and never stored. The audit step ignores an update that changes only `updated_at` (D23). *Test:* T-F02, T-D22 (one case through phpMyAdmin's own row editor). *Source:* F7; docs #8.
- **S7.** Nobody deletes. The API never issues `DELETE`, `TRUNCATE` or `DROP` (the one statement wrapper refuses them and a test lists every hit in the code), **and** the one database user the API uses has the **Delete** and **Drop** boxes unticked in hPanel (*Change Permissions*), so MariaDB itself refuses `DELETE`, `DROP TABLE` and `TRUNCATE` (which needs Drop, docs #3) for everyone who uses that user, Harsh's phpMyAdmin included [H][S]. Rows are archived, voided or cancelled, never removed; the few things that used to be deleted are changed instead: an old idempotency row has its result emptied (L6), a session is marked revoked (U4), and a photo the owner removes keeps its row but loses its bytes (P3). Schema changes keep Alter, Create, Index, References and Trigger ticked (A3 lists all 18 boxes). The same missing right means the user also cannot `DROP TABLE` (even `IF EXISTS`), `DROP`, `ALTER` or `CREATE OR REPLACE` a view, `RENAME TABLE`, `ALTER TABLE ... RENAME TO`, `REPLACE INTO` or `TRUNCATE`: all are refused with error 1142 [W]. S9 turns that into the rule for migrations, and section 11 into the choice of queries instead of views (a view can be created once and never changed). Only Harsh, in an emergency, can tick Delete for the time it takes (the runbook, B5, names the one case planned for: a purge of the housekeeping tables if they ever fill the plan, I12; the login counters of D24 are updated in place and never grow, so an attack on the login does not need it). (`NODE-DESIGN.md` section 8 item 2, as reviewed, keeps Delete ticked, blocks deletes on business tables with triggers and falls back to a second database for the housekeeping tables; draft 4 does not, because triggers are unproved on the plan, a second database cannot share a transaction with the money tables, and the three housekeeping tables can be kept tidy by `UPDATE`; 18.10.1 and O51.) *Test:* T-D07, T-C02, T-F04. *Source:* LED-03, DAT-01, INV-09; docs #3, #12.
- **S8.** Errors from the service functions are raised as `JV_*` codes and answered as a JSON body `{"error":{"code","message","details"},"request_id"}` with an HTTP status: `JV_INVALID_INPUT` 422; `JV_FORBIDDEN` 403; `JV_NOT_FOUND` 404; `JV_PIECE_UNAVAILABLE`, `JV_CUSTOMER_ARCHIVED`, `JV_OVERPAYMENT`, `JV_CASH_LIMIT`, `JV_FINE_EXCEEDS`, `JV_BILL_CANCELLED`, `JV_ALREADY_VOID`, `JV_NOT_ALLOWED` 409; `JV_BUSY` 503 with `Retry-After: 1`. Codes new in draft 4, because the API layer now exists: `JV_STALE` 409 (an edit made on an old copy; the body carries the current row, A5); `JV_UNAUTHENTICATED`, `JV_SESSION_EXPIRED`, `JV_BAD_LOGIN` 401; `JV_PASSWORD_CHANGE_REQUIRED` 403; `JV_CSRF` 403; `JV_RATE_LIMITED` 429 with `Retry-After`; `JV_PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE` 413 and `JV_UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE` 415 (photos); `JV_UNAVAILABLE` 503 (the start-up check failed, S11); `JV_INTERNAL` 500 (anything unexpected; the detail goes to the log with the request id, never to the client). The screens map each code to a plain message. What each database error becomes: a lock wait timeout (1205), a statement timeout (1969), a deadlock that still happens after the API's own retries (1213, S12), a full connection pool, a refused new connection (`connect EPERM`, S11) or a lost connection all become `JV_BUSY`, and nothing was changed, **with one exception no code can rule out**: a connection that drops during the final `COMMIT` leaves it unknown whether the work was saved. `JV_BUSY` therefore never promises "nothing was changed": the screen retries once with the same Idempotency-Key, the stored result of L5 tells the truth, and if the retry also fails the screen re-reads the bill or receipt before it says anything (C13). The handler timer of A14 (25 seconds) is longer than the deadline and the statement limit together (S12 (3)), so it never answers before the work has stopped; a duplicate key (1062) on a business key such as a `design_no` is `JV_INVALID_INPUT`, and on anything else it is a bug (`JV_INTERNAL`), because the idempotency claim and the numbering step are written so that they never raise it (L3, L5); a failed `CHECK` or an out-of-range value (4025, 1264) means the API's own validation missed something: answered `JV_INVALID_INPUT` and logged as a bug; a foreign-key error (1451, 1452) and anything else is `JV_INTERNAL`. For `JV_BUSY` the screen retries **once automatically with the same Idempotency-Key**, which is safe because of L5. *Test:* T-C04, T-C15, T-C19, T-E04 and each function test. *Source:* docs #1, #2; SPIKE (a), (e); NODE-DESIGN 4.5, 5.6.
- **S9.** Migrations are forward-only, **create-only** SQL files, one concern per file, run by a small runner that records each file in a `schema_migrations` table. **MariaDB DDL is not transactional**: every `CREATE`, `ALTER` and `DROP` commits at once and a rolled-back `CREATE TABLE` stays (docs #3, [S]), so each file must be **re-runnable on an empty database and on a half-applied one**: `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS`, `ADD COLUMN IF NOT EXISTS`, `CREATE INDEX IF NOT EXISTS` (all tested [S]); a file that fails half-way is run again, not repaired by hand. **What a migration may hold** was tried against a user with the 18 hPanel boxes minus Delete and Drop on MariaDB 11.8.9 [W]. *Allowed and used:* `CREATE TABLE [IF NOT EXISTS]`; `CREATE INDEX [IF NOT EXISTS]`; `ALTER TABLE` with `ADD COLUMN [IF NOT EXISTS]`, `ADD INDEX`, `ADD UNIQUE`, `ADD CONSTRAINT` (`CHECK` or `FOREIGN KEY`) and `MODIFY COLUMN` (only to widen or relax, never to narrow); `DROP CONSTRAINT`, `DROP FOREIGN KEY` and `DROP INDEX`, only to put a right rule in the place of a wrong one in the same file; `CREATE TRIGGER` and `DROP TRIGGER IF EXISTS`, in trigger files only (O50); seed rows by `INSERT IGNORE` or `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE`; `ANALYZE TABLE`. *Refused by MariaDB itself* (error 1142, because Drop and Delete are off, S7): `DROP TABLE` even with `IF EXISTS`; `DROP VIEW`; `RENAME TABLE`; `ALTER TABLE ... RENAME TO`; `CREATE OR REPLACE TABLE` and `CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW`; `ALTER VIEW`; `REPLACE INTO`; `TRUNCATE`; `DELETE`. *Allowed by the database but refused by the runner* as a rule of this spec, because they destroy or hide data: `ALTER TABLE ... DROP COLUMN` and `RENAME COLUMN` (a column that is no longer wanted stays, unused, with a comment in the file). The runner reads each file before it runs anything and refuses a statement outside the allowed list. A view can be created once and never changed, so no view is used (section 11, RM4-3). There is no row-level-security clause to add: a new table needs only its primary key, its foreign keys (`RESTRICT`) and its `CHECK`s, which the catalogue test requires (A7). How a migration reaches Hostinger's database is open (O49). *Test:* T-F04 (every allowed form runs as the restricted user, every refused form is refused by the runner and, sent directly, by MariaDB). *Source:* docs #3.
- **S10.** The role of the caller is read in one place only, the route wrapper of A1 and A6 (it looks the user up in `users`, A2); no handler contains a role string. *Test:* T-D10. *Source:* F20, NODE-DESIGN 3.1.
- **S11.** *(new in draft 4)* **Connection rules.** Every connection checkout starts with `SET SESSION` for: `transaction_isolation = 'READ-COMMITTED'`; a strict `sql_mode` (at least `STRICT_ALL_TABLES` and `ERROR_FOR_DIVISION_BY_ZERO`); `time_zone = '+00:00'`; `innodb_lock_wait_timeout = 5`; `max_statement_time = 8` (the hourly tick sets 20 for its own sessions, I8); and the character set `utf8mb4`. The API never relies on Hostinger's globals, which are: not strict, READ-COMMITTED, lock wait 50 s, statements cut at 120 s, idle connections closed after 20 s [H][P]. Only session settings are possible, because the user is not SUPER [S]. **There is one pool, of at most 8 connections.** Hostinger allows 75 open connections per user [H], but it also refuses a burst of **new** connections: the 21st new connection inside about a second fails with `connect EPERM` while 20 open at once work [P], so the pool of 8 opens at most 8 in a second after a quiet spell, and two processes that are alive together while a deploy overlaps open at most 16. **The running app opens connections only through this pool**: the hourly tick, the nightly check, the export and the checks of a start take theirs from it (the tick's `GET_LOCK` connection is one of the 8). A refused new connection is retried once after 1.1 seconds by the checkout and then answered `JV_BUSY` (S8). Scripts and tools that open many connections (the import, the restore tool, tests against staging) pace themselves to at most 10 new connections in any 1.2 seconds. The pool hands idle connections back after 10 seconds, before the server's 20-second cut, and keeps at most 2 idle; it never trusts an idle one: if the checkout statement finds the connection dead it is destroyed and another is checked out (up to twice; nothing had run yet), a connection the server closed is otherwise replaced without an error, and a connection killed inside a transaction fails its next statement and its `ROLLBACK`, and nothing is committed [S]. Sessions set `wait_timeout` to **300** for themselves (a normal user may): a plain connection was closed after 25 seconds idle, one with 300 stayed open, and a pool answered after 25 seconds idle by opening a new connection without an error [P]. The app reaches the database at `127.0.0.1`; the remote name `srv2210.hstgr.io` is refused for the app (error 1045) [P]. A **start-up check** runs before the API accepts a request: the server is MariaDB 11.x; the isolation level is READ-COMMITTED; the session settings above can be set; the character set is `utf8mb4`; every table the code needs exists; and the user's grants are read with `SHOW GRANTS` and reported (a Delete or Drop right that is on does not stop the API but turns the owner's dashboard line amber, I12). The check is quick, because it runs on every start after an idle stop (H7). If any other check fails the API still starts, logs why, and answers `503 JV_UNAVAILABLE` to everything except `/healthz`. *Test:* T-F08, T-C18. *Source:* docs #1, #2, #4, #5; HOSTINGER-FACTS 2; SPIKE section 3 rule 1; NODE-DESIGN 5.2.
- **S12.** *(new in draft 4)* **One request, one connection, one transaction** (the wrapper `withTx` that every writing call uses). (1) Any error means `ROLLBACK` of the whole transaction, whatever the error: a lock wait timeout (1205) rolls back only the failing statement, so earlier statements would still commit if the code carried on (docs #1, [S]); domain code therefore never catches a database error and continues (there is no exception: the idempotency claim and the numbering step never raise a duplicate-key error, L3, L5). (2) A deadlock (1213) re-runs the **whole** function up to 2 more times after 20 to 80 ms of jitter and then answers `JV_BUSY`; the idempotency row, the number and every other change were rolled back together with it, so the retry is safe. (3) The whole call has a **deadline of 12 seconds**, checked before every statement, because `max_statement_time` limits one statement and does not add up across statements [S]; with the statement limit of 8 seconds in S11 a transaction is over after at most 20 seconds, so no row is stamped more than 20 seconds before its commit (inside the 30-second overlap of C4) and the handler timer of A14 (25 seconds) always fires after the work has stopped. (4) Statement parameters are text, numbers, booleans, null or Buffers only: ordinary statements are prepared statements (`execute()`, one `?` for each value), an object parameter is refused, because mysql2's `query()` expands an object into `col = val` pairs [S]; JSON columns are always sent as `JSON.stringify` text and money inside JSON is a string; `REPLACE INTO` is never used (it deletes and inserts silently). (5) No network call (storage, mail, another API) happens inside a writing transaction, because it would hold row locks; there is no exception: the backup job writes its export to a temporary file inside the read-only snapshot, closes the snapshot, and only then compresses, encrypts and writes it to the backup folder (B2). (6) Money is integers in the API and `DECIMAL` text in SQL (S3). *Test:* T-C19, T-C15. *Source:* docs #1, #5; SPIKE (a), (e), section 3; NODE-DESIGN 5.1, 5.3, 5.7.

# PART A: FOUNDATION

## 2. Data model

All tables are in the one MariaDB database (InnoDB, character set `utf8mb4` and the collation `utf8mb4_unicode_ci` **named in every `CREATE TABLE`**, because a fresh 11.8.9 server defaults to `utf8mb4_uca1400_ai_ci` [W] while Hostinger's default is `utf8mb4_unicode_ci` [H]; with it a `UNIQUE` on text ignores case and trailing spaces [S]; table names are case-sensitive on Hostinger [H], so the code uses them exactly as written). "FK" means a foreign key with `ON DELETE RESTRICT` (nothing is ever deleted, S7). Column names are given in the database's `snake_case`; the screens keep their `camelCase` fields through the cache layer (C1). Type names are MariaDB's: draft 3's `numeric` is `DECIMAL`, `uuid` is `UUID`, `jsonb` is `JSON` (an alias of `LONGTEXT`; a `JSON_VALID` check is added), `timestamptz` is `DATETIME(6)` in UTC, `text[]` is a `JSON` array with `CHECK (JSON_TYPE(col) = 'ARRAY')`, and `boolean` is `TINYINT(1)` (docs #8). Names that are reserved words in MariaDB are not used unquoted [S]: `settings.key` is `setting_key`, and no table is called `lines`, `rows` or `order`.

### 2.1 Reference and settings

- **D1. `users`** (draft 3's `profiles`; the API's own accounts, with no outside login service): `id UUID` primary key; `email` (unique ignoring case; the login name); `full_name` (not null); `role` (`owner`, `biller`, `staff`; a `CHECK`); `active boolean not null default false`; `password_hash` (a self-describing Argon2id or scrypt string, U1); `must_change_password boolean not null default false` (the admin door sets it, U3); `last_login_at`; `created_at`, `updated_at`. A person who has a `users` row is never deleted (S7): retire them with `active = false`; the person columns of the other tables are plain UUIDs, so deleting the row would erase the only record of who that person was. A user made through the admin door (U3) is active and must change the temporary password at the first login (U11); a row inserted by hand is inactive unless `active` is set. Every active user may read every user's `id`, `full_name`, `role` and `active`, **never the hash** (so the screens can show who created or changed something); only the owner may update one (A8). *Test:* T-D06, T-E01. *Source:* SEC-02, SEC-04, review "created by names"; PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md D1.
- **D2. Role lookup.** On every request the API turns the session cookie into a `sessions` row and then into the `users` row (D24); the role and the `active` flag come from that read and never from the cookie or the request. A missing, expired or ended session, or a user who is not active, is answered `401` and nothing else is read or written (A10). This is the only place that turns a session into a role (S10). *Test:* T-D06, T-D10. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.3, 3.1.
- **D3. `settings`**: `setting_key varchar primary key` (`key` is reserved and fails unquoted [S]), `value JSON not null` (written with `JSON.stringify`, read back as an object), `updated_by UUID`. One row per key, never one blob. Keys: `business` (legal and display name, location, owner name, phone, address, GSTIN, tagline), `estimate` (number prefix, round-off flag, footer note), `taxes`, `tag_template`, `numbering` (design and piece padding, barcode prefix, category prefixes), `materials` (names and purities), `locations` (the four stock locations of `EditStockClient.tsx:13`: Main Showroom, Jaishri Vault, Workshop, Exhibition), `custom_fields`, `photos` (the photo rules of P1 and P2: at most 3 per design, longest side 1000 px, JPEG quality 72, thumbnail 320 px, 2 MiB), `data_epoch` (a timestamp text that bulk database work changes, C4), `last_download` (the owner's last Download everything: when, by whom and the row counts of the zip, B3; absent until the first one). The prototype's `users` list moves to `users` (D1); its `defaultGoldRate` and `defaultSilverRate` move to `daily_rates`. *Test:* T-F02. *Source:* `settingsStore.ts:36-82`, hazard 7, AC3.
- **D4. `daily_rates`**: `id`, `material` (`Gold`/`Silver`), `purity text`, `rate_per_gram DECIMAL(12,2)` (greater than 0), `rate_date date`, `confirmed_by UUID` (forced, S6); unique on (`rate_date`, `material`, `purity`). The rate a bill screen pre-fills for a material and purity is the row with the latest `rate_date` not after the bill's date; if there is none, the screen asks for one (X1 returns the warning `RATE_MISSING`); if the latest one is older than the bill's date it is still pre-filled and the screen shows a warning (X1 returns `RATE_STALE`, and the bill still saves, T-A17). The owner may add a rate for any date up to today and correct a row dated today (an audited update); a biller may add a rate dated today, once per material and purity (an API rule that compares `rate_date` with today's business date, S5; a `CHECK` cannot read the clock, S4). A purity with no rate row (for example `Custom`) needs a typed rate (O27). *Test:* T-A13, T-A17, T-D05. *Source:* BIL-10, CMP-05, `settingsStore.ts:109`, `DashboardRateCard.tsx:25`, F7, F14.
- **D5. `categories`** (`name` unique ignoring case, `prefix` 2 to 6 capitals or digits, `active`) and **`subcategories`** (`category_id` FK, `name`, unique per category ignoring case, `active`). Seeded from `DEFAULT_CATEGORIES`, `DEFAULT_CATEGORY_PREFIXES` and `DEFAULT_SUBCATEGORY_LIST` (`catalogStore.ts`). *Test:* T-F06. *Source:* `catalogStore.ts`.

### 2.2 Stock

- **D6. `designs`**: `id`; `design_no text not null` (unique ignoring case; from a counter for new designs, L3; her own code when imported, IM1); `name text`; `category_id` FK; `subcategory_id` FK null; **`material`, `purity` and `selling_method` live here and only here**; `material` is `Gold` or `Silver` (a `CHECK`: the prototype's type, `daily_rates` and the pricing rules R4 and R5 know only those two, and the import writes designs straight into the table); `description`, `notes`, `tags` (a JSON array); `primary_photo_id UUID` null and `photo_ids` (a JSON array of photo ids, P4); `archived_at`; `import_batch_id`. There is no design-level labour or piroi (the prototype copied the first piece's values into the design, `AddDesignClient.tsx:1160-1164`). **`selling_method` cannot be changed while the design has any non-archived piece**, because the piece rates mean rupees per gram for By Weight and a piece price for Per Piece (D7); the API refuses it (a rule in code; a trigger version is possible if triggers work, O48). *Test:* T-F01, T-F05. *Source:* INV-02, QA theme 12, F13.
- **D7. `skus`** (a physical piece): `id`; `design_id` FK; `sku_no text not null` and `barcode text not null`, each unique ignoring case (the API trims them: trailing spaces collide on this collation [S]); `gross_weight` and `customer_net_weight` (`DECIMAL(12,3)`, not negative); an API rule that runs on insert and on an update of the weight columns, and not on a status-only update, also refuses a customer net weight above the gross weight **when the gross weight is above 0** (a blank gross weight, the prototype's default, is stored as 0 and allowed) and, for a By Weight design, a customer net weight of 0 (O37); it is not a `CHECK`, because a `CHECK` runs on every update and would block a status change on an imported row that already breaks the rule; `stones`, `stone_weight`, `kundan`, `kundan_weight`; `piroi DECIMAL(12,2)`; `labour_retail`, `labour_semi`, `labour_wholesale` (`DECIMAL(12,2)`): for a **By Weight** design these are rupees per gram of NW, for a **Per Piece** design they are the piece price (as coded, `EditStockClient.tsx:17-23`); `status` (D8); `location text`; `notes`; `sold_bill_id UUID` (FK to `bills`, null unless Sold); `archived_at`; `import_batch_id`. A piece has no `material`, `purity`, `category` or `selling_method` of its own. `sku_no`, `barcode` and `design_id` never change after creation. *Test:* T-F01, T-F02, T-F06. *Source:* INV-02, INV-03, hazard 2.
- **D8. Piece status** is one of the ten as coded (`mockData.ts:1-2`): `In Stock`, `Reserved`, `On Approval`, `Sold`, `Returned`, `Repair`, `Transferred`, `Missing`, `Damaged`, `Archived`. Two table constraints: `(status = 'Sold') = (sold_bill_id is not null)` and `(status = 'Archived') = (archived_at is not null)`. A piece is archived only through X9 (which sets both together). *Test:* T-C04, T-F02, T-C08. *Source:* INV-01, BIL-11, F7.
- **D9. `sku_private`**: `sku_id UUID` primary key and FK to `skus`; `original_net_weight DECIMAL(12,3)`, not negative. It is a **separate table** so that "who can read the original weight" is a route rule and not a screen rule: only the owner-only route `sku-private` returns it, no response for a biller or staff contains it, and the bill snapshots never carry it (D12). Only the owner can read, insert or update it; `create_design` writes it only when the caller is the owner (X8). *Test:* T-D03, T-D04. *Source:* INV-10, SEC-03, `permissions.ts`, F7.

### 2.3 Customers, bills, receipts

- **D10. `customers`**: `id`; `name` (1 to 120 characters); `mobile` (required for a named customer; **a new rule that replaces the two in the code**, where `CustomerSearchPanel.tsx:42-44` only checks that it is not blank and `CustomerLedgerClient.tsx:50` wants exactly ten digits: ten digits, or `+` and 7 to 15 digits for a foreign number, spaces and dashes removed on entry; blank only for an imported customer, whom the screen asks for the number when selected; O33); `customer_type` (`Retail`, `Semi-Wholesale`, `Wholesale`, default `Retail`); `email`, `address`, `gst_number`, `pan` (a `CHECK` of five capital letters, four digits and one capital letter, Z3, written with a binary comparison, `BINARY pan REGEXP '^[A-Z]{5}[0-9]{4}[A-Z]$'`, because a plain `REGEXP` check is case-insensitive on the default collation and would accept `abcde1234f` [S]), `notes`; `is_walkin boolean`; `archived_at`; `import_batch_id`. Exactly one row has `is_walkin = true`: MariaDB has no partial index, so a generated column `walkin_key` (1 when `is_walkin` is true, NULL otherwise; `PERSISTENT`) carries a `UNIQUE` [S], which is safe because `is_walkin` never changes (D19); the row is seeded with a fixed id, can never be archived and never holds a `pan`. No balance column exists: balances are derived (V1 to V3). *Test:* T-B17, T-C02, T-F06. *Source:* BIL-01, LED-07, DAT-01, `mockData.ts:63-78`, AC5.
- **D11. `bills`**: `id`; `bill_no text unique not null`; `customer_id` FK; `bill_date date`; `doc_type` (`estimate` as coded; `tax_invoice`, `bill_of_supply` reserved for decision 4; `opening_balance` for dues brought forward); `sale_type`; `status` (`active`, `cancelled`); `cancel_reason`, `cancelled_at`, `cancelled_on date` (the business day of the cancel, taken from the API clock in `Asia/Kolkata` like `bill_date`, S5, so that V3 and V4 never convert a UTC stamp into a day), `cancelled_by` (all present exactly when cancelled); `reissue_of UUID` (unique, FK to `bills`: the cancelled bill this one replaces, X10); `pricing_version smallint not null default 1` (R13); `subtotal`, `discount`, `additional_charges`, `gst_percent`, `gst_amount`, `grand_total` (money, not negative; `gst_*` are 0 for estimates as coded); **`round_off DECIMAL(14,2)` which may be negative** (R8); `fine_weight_due`; `notes`; `cash_override_reason text`; `import_batch_id`; `request_id UUID unique`. **No route inserts, updates or deletes a bill directly**; only the service functions in Part B write it. *Test:* T-D08, T-C05, T-A10. *Source:* BIL-12, CMP-09, F8, F11.
- **D12. `bill_items`**: `id`; `bill_id` FK; `line_no`; `sku_id` FK; a **snapshot** taken at sale time: `design_no`, `sku_no`, `category`, `material`, `purity`, `selling_method`, `gross_weight`, `customer_net_weight`; the priced values: `quantity integer`, `metal_rate`, `labour_rate`, `labour_amount`, `piroi`, `other_charges`, `metal_amount`, `fine_weight_due`, `item_total`, `tax_rate`, `tax_amount`; and the **list prices** the screen offered: `list_metal_rate` and `list_labour_rate` (so a price changed at the counter can be reported, I11). Unique on (`bill_id`, `line_no`) and on (`bill_id`, `sku_id`). The snapshot never contains the original weight. Quantity is stored (the prototype lost it, `BillingSummaryPanel.tsx:95-115`). *Test:* T-A06, T-D03, T-B24. *Source:* BIL-05, INV-10, F12.
- **D13. `receipts`**: `id`; `customer_id` FK; `kind` (`money`, `fine_silver`, `fine_cash`, `refund`); `mode` (money and refund: `Cash`, `UPI`, `Card`, `Bank Transfer`, `Other`; fine_cash: `Cash`; fine_silver: none); `amount` (money, fine_cash, refund: greater than 0; fine_silver: 0); `fine_grams` (fine kinds: greater than 0; others: 0); `silver_rate` (fine_cash only, greater than 0); `paid_on date`; `reference`, `notes`; `is_opening boolean` (an advance brought forward, D15); `cash_override_reason text` (Z1); `status` (`active`, `voided`); `void_reason`, `voided_at`, `voided_on date` (the business day of the void, from the API clock like `paid_on`, S5), `voided_by` (all present exactly when voided). A receipt has no `request_id` of its own: one request can make several receipts (a bill paid by cash and UPI, or an opening money advance and a silver one), and the idempotency table of L5 guards repeats. A `refund` is money **handed back** to the customer; it has no allocations. **There is no `Credit` mode**: leaving a bill unpaid is the absence of a receipt (as coded `BillingSummaryPanel.tsx:285` offers "Credit" as a payment row; it is counted as money received, BIL-03). Checks tie `kind` to the other columns. No route writes receipts directly. *Test:* T-B01, T-D08, T-B20. *Source:* BIL-03, LED-11, F2.
- **D14. `receipt_allocations`**: `id`; `receipt_id`; `bill_id`; `customer_id` (denormalised so that composite foreign keys `(receipt_id, customer_id)` and `(bill_id, customer_id)` make it **impossible** for a receipt to touch another customer's bill; InnoDB refuses the foreign key (error 1005, errno 150) unless the parent has an index that starts with (`id`, `customer_id`) [W], so `bills` and `receipts` each carry a `UNIQUE (id, customer_id)`); `amount DECIMAL(14,2)` and `fine_grams DECIMAL(12,3)` (not negative, not both zero); `released_at DATETIME(6)` (set when the bill is cancelled; a released allocation no longer counts). *Test:* T-B06. *Source:* LED-01, LED-04.
- **D15. Opening balances** are ordinary rows. What a customer owed on the first day becomes one `bills` row with `doc_type = 'opening_balance'`, no lines, `grand_total` and `fine_weight_due` set to the figures, dated the cut-over day, numbered `OB-nnnn`. What a customer had paid in advance becomes a `receipts` row with `is_opening = true` (kind `money` with mode `Other`, or `fine_silver` with no mode; reference "Opening advance"). Both are made by X12 or by the import (IM5). Payments, "oldest first", pending and the ledger then work with no special case. Opening rows are **not sales** and **not money received today**: they are excluded from sales figures, from cash totals (V4) and from the cash limit (Z1). *Test:* T-B05, T-B21. *Source:* design decision (O8), F4.

### 2.4 Audit, checks and private tables

- **D16. `audit_log`**: `id BIGINT AUTO_INCREMENT` (not a human number, so a gap is harmless); `at DATETIME(6)`; `actor UUID`; `actor_role text` (a role, or `import`, `admin-cli`, `job`, `sql`); `request_id UUID` (the request that made the change); `table_name`; `row_id text`; `action` (`insert`, `update`, or `event` for a login, a **summary of failed logins** (one row for each hour in which there were any, never one for each attempt, D24), a password change, an admin-door call or a job run, which carry their facts in `new_row`); `old_row JSON`; `new_row JSON`. Append-only (L2). *Test:* T-D14.
- **D17. `check_runs`** (`id`, `ran_at`, `ok boolean`, `details JSON`: the nightly checks, I8) and **`backup_runs`** (`id`, `ran_at`, `kind` (`db_dump`, `photo_copy`, `restore_drill`), `ok boolean`, `bytes bigint`, `detail JSON` with the counts of the run, where it stopped and the size of the backup folder): the backup job writes a row with `ok` true only when a phase is **complete** for the day and a row with `ok` false when it fails, never an `ok` for a partial slice (I8, B2). Both are read by the owner only. *Test:* T-B18, T-R03.
- **D18. `sys_counters`** (`series varchar primary key`, `last_value bigint`, `seed_value bigint default 0`), **`sys_idempotency`** (`request_id UUID primary key`, `fn`, `actor`, `args_hash`, `claim_token`, `result JSON`, `created_at`) and **`sys_import_batches`** (`id UUID`, `name text unique`, `source_file`, `row_counts JSON`, `created_at`). The caps of S4, the cash and PAN thresholds of section 13 and the review thresholds of I11 (`discount_flag_percent` 10 and `rate_flag_percent` 5) are the API's constants file `limits`, **not a table** (draft 3 kept them in `private.limits`; T-F02 compares the `CHECK` literals with the constants). No route returns any of these tables (S1). *Test:* T-D01, T-D16.
- **D19. Indexes and keys** (every column a join, a poll or a locking statement uses): `bills (customer_id, bill_date)`, `bills (updated_at, id)`, `bill_items (bill_id)`, `skus (design_id)`, `skus (status)`, `skus (updated_at, id)`, `designs (updated_at, id)`, and the same pair (`updated_at`, primary key) on every other table the screens poll (`sku_private`, `bill_items`, `receipt_allocations`, `daily_rates`, `settings`, `categories`, `subcategories`, `users`), `receipts (customer_id)`, `receipts (updated_at, id)`, `receipt_allocations (bill_id)`, `receipt_allocations (receipt_id)`, `receipt_allocations (customer_id)` (the balance of one customer reads by it, V2), `design_photos (design_id)` (the three-photo count of P2 and the sweep of P6), `customers (updated_at, id)`, `audit_log (table_name, row_id)`, and for the internal tables `sys_idempotency (created_at)`, `sessions (token_hash)` (unique), `sessions (user_id)`, `sessions (expires_at)`, `sys_job_runs (job, run_date)` (unique), `sys_photo_copies (photo_id, kind)` (its primary key) and `sys_login_attempts (scope, slot)` (its primary key and its only index, D24). MariaDB creates an index for every foreign key by itself [S]. Two rules that come from the spike: (1) **every `UPDATE`, `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` and keyed read in the code is backed by an index**: an `UPDATE ... WHERE` on a column with no index scans, locks what it scans and deadlocked 12 to 32 times in 1,200 operations even with the right lock order [S], so, for example, the pieces of a cancelled bill are freed by the ids already locked, never by `WHERE sold_bill_id = ?`; (2) **no `UNIQUE` index on a column that changes after insert**: a unique generated column that went NULL when a bill was cancelled ("one live line per piece") deadlocked against the number counter in 2 of about 6 runs [S], so "no piece on two active bills" stays invariant I2 with the piece lock of K2; the immutable unique columns (`design_no`, `sku_no`, `barcode`, `bill_no`, `request_id`, `reissue_of`, `walkin_key`) are fine. *Test:* the `EXPLAIN` test and the catalogue test (T-D11, which also lists every unique key). *Source:* docs #8; SPIKE (e) F1, F2.
- **D20. Seeds on an empty project:** `settings` rows from `DEFAULT_SETTINGS`, the categories and prefixes, the walk-in customer, and nothing else. **No sample data is ever seeded or substituted**: the prototype replaced unreadable stored data with `MOCK_*` records and the next save erased the real ones (`appStore.ts:105`, `designStore.ts:11-20`). *Test:* T-E05, T-F06. *Source:* DAT-05, QA theme 11.
- **D21. Import columns.** `designs`, `skus`, `customers` and `bills` have a nullable `import_batch_id` (FK to `sys_import_batches`) so every imported row can be listed and checked. *Test:* T-C16. *Source:* F4.
- **D22. Forced person columns.** The API sets `created_by`, `updated_by`, `set_by` and `confirmed_by` from the session user in the same statement as the change, and leaves them null for the import (IM2); no request field can name them (an unknown field is refused, A5). A trigger using a session variable would also work if Hostinger allows triggers [S], but draft 4 does not depend on it (O48). *Test:* T-D22. *Source:* F7.
- **D23. Touches (for polling).** The screens poll by `updated_at` (C4), but balances change when a row other than the customer's own changes. So the service function that inserts or updates `receipts` or `receipt_allocations` also sets `updated_at` (from the API clock) on the affected `bills` rows and on the customer, in the same transaction and under the customer lock it already holds; a bill insert, and an update of a bill's `status` (a cancel, including a cancel with nothing allocated), does the same for the customer. The audit step skips an update that changes nothing but `updated_at`, so these touches add no audit noise. (A trigger would also work but hides a lock, and a trigger cannot update its own table, error 1442 [S].) *Test:* T-E07, T-D14. *Source:* F3, RF-1.
- **D24.** *(new in draft 4; the login counters were rewritten in draft 5)* **`sessions`** (`id UUID` primary key; `user_id` FK to `users`; `token_hash` (SHA-256 of the cookie ticket, unique; the ticket itself is never stored); `created_at`, `last_seen_at`, `expires_at`; `revoked_at` null; `user_agent`) and **`sys_login_attempts`**, the login counters, which are **updated in place and never grow**: `scope` (`email`, `addr` or `any`), `slot smallint`, `window_start DATETIME(6)`, `n int`, primary key (`scope`, `slot`); one more row (`scope` `audit`, `slot` 0) keeps the failure count of the current hour. The slot is the first two bytes of the SHA-256 of a secret salt (an environment variable, U10), a bar and the lower-cased e-mail (scope `email`) or the visitor's address (scope `addr`), taken modulo 1,024, so the table can never hold more than 2,050 rows whatever is sent: 20,000 different invented e-mails filled 1,024 rows and no more, in 16 KB [W]. Two e-mails that share a slot share a counter, which costs both a delay of at most 8 seconds and never a lock (A13); an invented e-mail is counted like a real one, so the screen cannot tell them apart. **An attempt is counted when the request arrives**, by one atomic statement, `INSERT INTO sys_login_attempts (scope, slot, window_start, n) VALUES (?, ?, UTC_TIMESTAMP(6), 1) ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE n = IF(window_start < UTC_TIMESTAMP(6) - INTERVAL ? SECOND, 1, n + 1), window_start = IF(window_start < UTC_TIMESTAMP(6) - INTERVAL ? SECOND, UTC_TIMESTAMP(6), window_start)` (900 seconds for scope `email`, 60 for `addr` and `any`), followed by a plain `SELECT n` of that row, so parallel attempts are counted before any password is checked and the count survives a restart. **Failure summary:** every failed attempt adds one to the `audit` row; the first failed attempt after the hour has changed claims the summary with one conditional `UPDATE ... WHERE window_start < ?` (exactly one request wins) and writes **one** `audit_log` event `login_failures` with the count of the hour that ended, so an attack makes at most 24 audit rows a day and none for the attempts themselves. **Worst case, by arithmetic and not measured on Hostinger:** draft 4 wrote a counter row and an audit row for every failed attempt, and the two hashing slots let about 6 attempts a second through with the scrypt fallback (348 ms a hash [W]) and about 12 with Argon2id (170 ms [P]), that is half a million to a million rows a day, roughly 300 to 600 MB a day at about 600 bytes a row with its indexes, for ever, because nothing can be deleted (S7); draft 5 holds at most 2,050 counter rows (about 0.3 MB, fixed) and at most 24 audit rows a day (about 15 KB a day, 5 MB a year) however long the attack lasts. No route returns either table. Nothing is deleted from `sessions` (S7): a session that ends is marked with `revoked_at` and stays; there is one row for each real login, a few thousand a year. *Test:* T-D16, T-D24, T-D25, T-D26, T-D30. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.3, 2.5, D-Q1; RF4-2.
- **D25.** *(new in draft 4; `sys_photo_copies` added in draft 5)* **`sys_job_runs`** (`job`, `run_date`, unique together; `status` (`running`, `ok`, `failed`); `started_at`, `finished_at`; `detail JSON`) makes each scheduled phase run once per business date (I8), **`sys_photo_copies`** (`photo_id UUID`, `kind`, `sha256`, `size`, `copied_at`; primary key `photo_id` and `kind`) holds one row for each photo object that is safely in the backup folder, written only after a `stat` of the file in the folder has confirmed the stored size, so that "copied once" (B2) is remembered (it has no foreign key: a removed photo keeps its copy, P3, B2), and **`sys_error_log`** (`at`, `level`, `code`, a short `message`, `request_id`, `user_id` and a `detail` that hold no customer data) keeps unexpected errors beyond Hostinger's log window of the latest deployment. *Test:* T-D16, T-R03, T-R04, T-D28. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 7.2, 7.3, D-Q1; RF4-8.
- **D26.** *(new in draft 4)* **`design_photos`**: `id UUID` and `kind` (`full` or `thumb`), together the primary key (one photo is a full image and its 320 px thumbnail under one id); `design_id UUID` with **no foreign key**, because the screen uploads before the design exists (P2), so the monthly sweep (P6) finds the photos that never got attached; `mime` (`image/jpeg`, `image/png` or `image/webp`); `bytes MEDIUMBLOB` (at most 16,777,215 bytes, docs #8, and 2 MiB by P1; it is NULL only once the photo is removed, P3); `byte_size` (equal to the length of `bytes` while it is present); `sha256` (32 bytes, of the bytes as uploaded); `removed_at` null (set when the owner removes the photo; the photo is then no longer served); `created_at`, `created_by`. A `CHECK` ties the two states: either `removed_at` is null and `bytes` is present with `byte_size` equal to its length, or `removed_at` is set and `bytes` is NULL. `designs` holds only the ids (`photo_ids`, D6). The nightly export leaves `bytes` out (B2), so a restore cannot load a live photo row first and fill the bytes later: the `CHECK` refuses it with error 4025 [W]; the restore tool inserts each live row together with its bytes (B4). **No list query ever selects `bytes`.** *Test:* T-D21, T-F10. *Source:* HOSTINGER-FACTS 3; decision of Harsh, 15 Oct 2026; NODE-DESIGN D-Q1.

## 3. Access rules (the API is the wall)

- **A1.** Every route declares who may call it. Every endpoint is registered through one wrapper (`route()`), which takes an `action` that exists in the permission matrix of A4 (the session calls `me`, `logout`, `change-password` and `activity` have a row of their own there), or sits on a short explicit list of public routes (`/healthz`, `/readyz`, `POST /v1/auth/login`, and the photo read of P3) or of token routes (`/admin/v1/*`, `/internal/jobs/*`, each with its own token check). A route registered without an action fails the build, and a test lists every registered route with its roles. There is **no row-level security**: MariaDB has one database user for every role (A3), so the database cannot tell a biller from an owner, and **the wall is the API's code and its tests**. (Harsh's phpMyAdmin was always a second door, for the database owner, in the old design too.) The matrix lives in one file and a handler never checks a role itself (S10). *Test:* T-D01, T-D02. *Source:* PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md A1; NODE-DESIGN 3.1, 3.4.
- **A2.** Roles come from `users` through the lookup of D2, on every request. No code reads a role from a cookie, a token, a header or a request body; the cookie carries only an opaque session ticket (U4), and a body or header that names a role is ignored or refused. *Test:* T-D06, T-D15. *Source:* SEC-02.
- **A3.** **One database user, rights narrowed in hPanel.** Hostinger creates one database user per database (the create form asks for the name, the user and the password together, and a second user for the same database is not offered [H]), so the API, the migration runner and Harsh's phpMyAdmin all use it. In hPanel (*Change Permissions*, 18 boxes: Select, Insert, Update, Delete, Create, Drop, References, Index, Alter, Create temporary tables, Lock tables, Execute, Create view, Show view, Create routine, Alter routine, Event and Trigger [H]) **Delete and Drop are unticked** (S7). **Ticked: Select, Insert, Update, Create, Alter, Index, References, Trigger, Create temporary tables and Lock tables** (the first eight are what the API, the migrations and the triggers of O50 use; the last two do no harm and phpMyAdmin may want them [U]). **Unticked besides Delete and Drop: Create view, Show view, Execute, Create routine, Alter routine and Event**, because this design uses no view (section 11), no stored routine (A6) and no event (the event scheduler is off on the plan [H]); the proof of O48 (test 3) runs with exactly this set and the start-up check reports any other right as a note in the log (S11). The user has no SUPER and no FILE and cannot grant, revoke or create users [S]. Table and column grants work in MariaDB [S] but would not separate the roles here, because every role goes through the same user, so the roles are separated by the API (A1, A4). The user's password exists in Hostinger's environment variables and in Harsh's password manager, and during a migration or import window also on his Mac (S1, U10); `.env` is never committed. A second, tighter user for the running API (select, insert and update only) with the migration user kept by Harsh alone would be better, but Hostinger offers one user per database [H]; if it ever offers two, the running API takes the tighter one (O49). *Test:* T-D02, T-D07, T-D29, T-F08. *Source:* HOSTINGER-FACTS 1, 2.4; PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md A3; RF4-10, RF4-11.
- **A4.** The permission matrix. It is one file with one row per thing a caller can do: each **read resource** (served by `GET /v1/sync/<resource>` and the named read calls of C3), each **direct edit** (`PATCH`/`PUT`/`POST` of ordinary fields, A5), each **service function** X1 to X12 with its route (section 10), and the photo calls (P3). The table below gives the read and edit rows (S = read, I = insert, U = update; "content columns" are listed in A5); a caller who is not signed in, or not `active`, gets nothing in any row. Each resource also has a **field allow-list per role** (`READ_COLUMNS`, `WRITE_COLUMNS`) in the same file: a read names its columns and never uses `SELECT *`, so a biller's customer row has mobile, GST and PAN as in A9 but no response to a biller or staff ever contains `original_net_weight`. **All read roles may read archived rows** (the screens hide them), because the screens and the polling need them to see an archiving. The four derived reads are queries of the API, not database views (section 11). The session calls `me`, `logout`, `change-password` and `activity` are rows of the matrix too, open to every signed-in user, including one who must still change the password (U11). Draft 3 gave this table to Postgres policies; the same expectations are now HTTP tests, one login per role: **a generated test walks every row of this table for each of the five callers** (no session, an ended session, an inactive user, staff, biller, owner) and every column of `READ_COLUMNS` and `WRITE_COLUMNS`, as T-D09 does for X1 to X12, so a wrong cell fails a test and is not only sampled (T-D02).

| Table or derived read | owner | biller | staff | anon |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `users` (id, full_name, role, active; never the hash) | S all; U `full_name`, `role`, `active` (not her own role or `active`; the screen is M2, A8) | S all | S all | none |
| `settings` | S, I, U | S all keys | S the keys `numbering`, `materials`, `locations`, `custom_fields`, `tag_template`, `photos` and `data_epoch` only | none |
| `daily_rates` | S, I (any date up to today), U (a row dated today) | S, I (today only) | S | none |
| `categories`, `subcategories` | S, I, U | S | S, I | none |
| `designs` | S, U content columns, `archived_at`, `material`, `purity`, `selling_method` | S | S, U content columns (`photo_ids` only by adding ids at the end, A5), `archived_at` | none |
| `skus` | S, U content columns | S | S, U content columns | none |
| `sku_private` | S, I, U | none | none | none |
| `customers` | S, U content columns, `archived_at` | S, U content columns | none | none |
| `bills`, `bill_items`, `receipts`, `receipt_allocations` | S | S | none | none |
| the four derived reads `bill_summary`, `customer_balances`, `customer_ledger`, `daily_totals` (queries, section 11) | S | S (`daily_totals` for today and yesterday only, O25) | none | none |
| `audit_log`, `check_runs`, `backup_runs` | S | none | none | none |
| the download record `POST /v1/owner/download-record` (B3) | I (once for each call) | none | none | none |
| the session calls `me`, `logout`, `change-password`, `activity` (U5, U8, U11) | every active user | every active user | every active user | none |

  Inserts into `designs`, `skus`, `customers`, `bills`, `bill_items`, `receipts` and `receipt_allocations` are **not offered as direct routes to any role**: only the service functions of section 10, and the import (IM1), write them (numbers, snapshots and balances cannot be forged by a client). Nobody has `DELETE` (S7). *Test:* T-D02 to T-D05, T-D07, T-D08, T-D18, T-D19. *Source:* hazards 1, 2, 4, SEC-02, INV-10, F6, F7.
- **A5.** Content columns can be edited directly: for `designs`: `name`, `description`, `notes`, `tags`, `primary_photo_id`, `photo_ids`, `category_id`, `subcategory_id`, `archived_at`; for `skus`: `gross_weight`, `customer_net_weight`, `stones`, `stone_weight`, `kundan`, `kundan_weight`, `piroi`, `labour_retail`, `labour_semi`, `labour_wholesale`, `location`, `notes`; for `customers`: `name`, `mobile`, `customer_type`, `email`, `address`, `gst_number`, `pan`, `notes`. A per-route input schema lists the editable fields per role (`WRITE_COLUMNS`): a field outside the list is refused with `JV_FORBIDDEN`, an unknown field with `JV_INVALID_INPUT`, and nothing is silently ignored (`skus.status` and `skus.archived_at` change only through X9). The API also rejects: staff changing a design's `material`, `purity` or `selling_method`; staff removing, reordering or replacing a design's `photo_ids` (they may only add ids at the end, and `primary_photo_id` must stay one of the ids; the owner may do all four, P3), and any `photo_ids` or `primary_photo_id` value that fails the checks of P2; a biller changing a customer's `archived_at`; archiving a customer whose balance, fine or advance is not zero; archiving a design that has non-archived pieces; archiving takes the same lock as a money function (the customer lock of X0 item 3 for a customer), so a payment cannot arrive between the zero-balance check and the archive. These are rules in code; draft 3 held them in triggers, and a trigger version is possible if triggers work (O48). A stock edit sends `expected_updated_at` (the value as the API returned it, as exact text) and **only the changed fields**; the API runs `UPDATE ... WHERE id = ? AND updated_at = ?`, and if no row changed it answers `409 JV_STALE` with the current row in `details.current`, so a stale edit changes nothing and the screen reloads; the screen keeps the value **as the exact text** and never turns it into a date (C4). No `Idempotency-Key` is needed for an edit: the same edit sent twice is refused the second time by the stale check. *Test:* T-C07, T-C20, T-D04, T-D19, T-D21, T-F05, T-F09. *Source:* hazard 2, INV-01, `EditStockClient.tsx:62-65`, F3, F7; NODE-DESIGN 4.4.
- **A6.** Service functions. Every writing call is a service function X1 to X12 or an ordinary edit (A5), reached only through `route()`: session, then the must-change-password gate (U11), then the matrix row, then the body validation (so a forbidden caller learns nothing about the body), then the handler. Each service function checks the role again at its start (X0 item 2: after the idempotency claim, whose row the rollback removes when the check refuses) and raises `JV_FORBIDDEN` otherwise (defence in depth). They are Node functions, not stored procedures: creating routines or triggers may need SUPER on Hostinger [U], they are harder to test, and the lock order is easier to keep in one module (S12, X0). `quote_bill` (X7) is also a service function; it reads prices and writes nothing. *Test:* T-D09, T-D10. *Source:* PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md A6; NODE-DESIGN 1.3, 3.1.
- **A7.** Static checks replace the Supabase advisor (MariaDB has no equivalent product). The build fails if: a route has no declared roles (A1); a table lacks a primary key, InnoDB or the collation `utf8mb4_unicode_ci`; a money column is not `DECIMAL` or any `FLOAT`/`DOUBLE` column exists; a foreign key is not `RESTRICT`; a named `CHECK` of D8, D13 or S4 is missing; the list of `UNIQUE` keys differs from the expected list (D19); a locking or writing statement does not use an index (the `EXPLAIN` test); the code contains a statement that starts with `DELETE`, `TRUNCATE`, `DROP` or `ALTER` outside the migration runner (S7); a handler contains a role string (S10); a migration file holds a statement outside the allowed list of S9; a bound money or weight parameter is used in SQL arithmetic, `ROUND` or a comparison without `CAST` (S3); the code opens a database connection outside the pool (S11); a runtime dependency has an install script or a High or Critical `npm audit` finding. Hostinger also scans npm packages after each deployment and may open a fix pull request that Harsh must review [N]. *Test:* T-D11. *Source:* PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md A7; NODE-DESIGN 8 items 3, 12.
- **A8.** The owner can change another user's `role` and `active` (the in-app staff screen is later; until then Harsh does it through the admin door, U3, which has a call to change a role or the `active` flag, audited with the actor `admin-cli`). **No user can change their own `role` or `active`**, owner included, so the owner cannot lock herself out or promote herself; the route refuses it. Biller and staff have no route that updates `users` at all. *Test:* T-D06, T-D20.
- **A9.** Reading is wide, writing is narrow: a role that may read a resource may read every row; the narrowing is by field allow-lists and functions, not by per-row ownership. This means the **biller reads every customer's mobile, GST and PAN and the whole ledger** and may edit customer details; this is as in the prototype and is listed for Siddhi in O25. The one place where the API is narrower than the prototype is the cash book (`daily_totals`): a biller reads today and yesterday only (A4, O25). *Test:* T-D04, T-D18. *Source:* matches the prototype; F12.
- **A10.** A user who is `active = false` or has no `users` row reads nothing and writes nothing: the lookup of D2 answers `401` on every call, **a session that is already open included**, so a user deactivated in the middle of a shift is cut off at the next call (this replaces draft 3's poll check, C18). *Test:* T-D06, T-D26, T-E01.
- **A11.** There is no GraphQL and no generic table route. The only data routes are the named ones of A4; a path that is not in the matrix, such as `/graphql` or `/v1/sync/sessions`, answers `404`, and no route accepts a table or column name from the client (the names come from the allow-lists). *Test:* T-D17. *Source:* F6; NODE-DESIGN 3.4, 8 item 6.
- **A12.** *(new in draft 4)* **Cross-origin and cross-site requests.** The website and the API are two subdomains of one registrable domain that is Siddhi's own (O38: a domain used only for JewelVault, with one name for the website and one for the API), so the calls are cross-origin but the session cookie is first-party (same-site). CORS answers only for an exact list of origins (the environment variable `ALLOWED_ORIGINS`; in production only the website's origin; never `*`, no patterns, no preview URLs), with credentials, the methods `GET`, `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH` and `OPTIONS` (the plugin's default is only `GET`, `HEAD` and `POST`, so an ordinary edit by `PATCH` would fail its preflight, RF4-19), the request headers `Content-Type`, `Idempotency-Key` and `X-JV-Client`, and the exposed response headers `Retry-After`, `X-Request-Id` and `Idempotent-Replay` (the screen cannot read them otherwise). Three independent CSRF layers: the cookie is `SameSite=Strict` (U12); every call that changes state (POST, PATCH, PUT, DELETE) must carry `X-JV-Client: web`, which makes the browser send a preflight that only our origin passes; and for such a call the `Origin` header must equal an allowed origin exactly (a missing or `null` Origin is refused). Any failure is `403 JV_CSRF`. No state change is reachable by GET. **Measured on the plan [P]:** two `hostingersite.com` addresses are the same site (that suffix is not on the Public Suffix List) and the whole flow of cookie, CORS and images passed between them in Chrome 154 and Safari 27.0.1, whereas `vercel.app` and `pages.dev` addresses are separate sites. A website on a `vercel.app` address with an API on any other address would therefore be cross-site and the `Strict` cookie would not be sent, so **every environment uses two names under one domain** (O38, O43); until Siddhi's domain is connected the build and staging use two `hostingersite.com` addresses. Safari may cap the age of a cookie set by a subdomain whose server differs from the main site's at 7 days [U]; that would mean a sign-in every week. If the cookie test on the real names fails (O48, test 4) the name pair is re-planned, or the API is put behind the website's own origin by a proxy rewrite, which Vercel offers (docs #11 area, `NODE-DESIGN.md` 2.2; Cloudflare Pages cannot proxy to an outside API [U]). *Test:* T-D23, T-E10. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.2, 2.6; docs #14 [N].
