- **A13.** *(new in draft 4; the login and photo rows rewritten in draft 5)* **Throttles.** **Login** has three caps and never a lock, all counted by D24 when the request *arrives*: (1) **per e-mail** (the counter slot of the e-mail, a 15-minute window): after 5 failures each further attempt for that e-mail waits 1, 2, 4 and then at most **8 seconds** before the password is checked, and at most **2 attempts for one e-mail may be waiting or running at a time** (a third gets `429 JV_RATE_LIMITED` at once); a correct password still works after the wait, so an attacker can slow the owner's login by 8 seconds and can never lock it; (2) **per address across all e-mails**: 20 attempts a minute, from the header `x-real-ip`, which Hostinger's front end sets to the visitor's address and overwrites when a client sends its own [P] (`x-forwarded-for` keeps a client's value, so it is not read and `trustProxy` stays off); if the address is ever missing, a global cap of 60 attempts a minute (scope `any`) applies instead; (3) **in flight**: at most 8 logins waiting or hashing at one time, then an immediate `429`; an unknown e-mail is counted and gets one dummy hash exactly like a known one, and this cap is what stops a flood of unknown e-mails from starving a real login on the two hashing slots (U1). The wait stops when the client has gone or the timer fires, and the password is not checked after that; **the login route has its own handler timer of 30 seconds** (A14); the screen never retries a login by itself. An attacker still gets about one guess every 4 seconds per e-mail (about 20,000 a day); the minimum length of 10, the common-password list and the local-part rule of U9 are what make that harmless. Change password: 5 per 15 minutes per user. Admin door: 10 a minute per address, and after two wrong tokens in a row a 15-minute pause for that address; if the address cannot be trusted the pause is replaced by a global limit of 5 wrong tokens an hour, so a stranger cannot lock Harsh out for long. Job tick: 6 a minute per address and 20 a minute in all (a wrong token must not get a fresh bucket each time). Photo upload: 30 a minute per user. **Photo read** (the one call with no session): 600 a minute per address; if the address cannot be trusted, a **global cap of 120 a second** applies instead, and every photo answer carries an `ETag` (the photo's sha256) so that `If-None-Match` is answered `304` without a database read. Everything else: 300 a minute per user (normal use is about 8 poll calls a minute [U]). Over the limit: `429 JV_RATE_LIMITED` with `Retry-After`. Every limit except the login rows (which are in the database) starts again from zero when the process restarts after an idle stop; that is accepted. *Test:* T-D24, T-D30. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.5 (second round), 7.3; RF4-1, RF4-2, RF4-30; the global photo cap is draft 5's own.
- **A14.** *(new in draft 4)* **Responses leak nothing.** `JV_INTERNAL` carries no SQL, stack or table name; every data response carries `Cache-Control: no-store` (the photo files excepted, P3) and `X-Request-Id`; the standard security headers are set (helmet); the logs redact cookies, authorization and password fields and never hold a mobile, PAN or GST number; the owner-only weight never appears in a response to a biller or staff (D9). A request body is limited to 1 MiB (a photo upload to 2 MiB and 16 KiB), unknown JSON keys are refused, receiving a request may take 30 seconds, and an ordinary call that takes longer than **25 seconds** is answered `503 JV_BUSY` by the handler timer (30 seconds for the login route, 50 seconds for the job tick, because Hostinger's front end cuts a request at about 60 seconds whatever the app does [P]); the timer is longer than the transaction deadline of 12 seconds plus the statement limit of 8 (S12), so the work has already stopped when it fires. `/healthz` and `/readyz` answer only `{"ok":true}` or `{"ok":false}`; the reason for a failed start-up check (a missing table, a wrong isolation level, a Delete right that is on) goes to `sys_error_log` and the owner's dashboard line and never to a stranger. *Test:* T-D28, T-D03. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 4.0, 8 items 7, 11.

## 4. Login, sessions, photos, audit and numbering

### 4.1 Login and sessions

- **U1.** Login is e-mail and password through the API's own route `POST /v1/auth/login`; there is no outside login service. The password is stored as a self-describing **Argon2id** hash made with Node's built-in `crypto.argon2` (docs #10), starting at 64 MiB of memory, 3 passes and parallelism 1 and tuned on Hostinger to roughly 150 to 400 ms a hash (the proof app measured 159 ms for 64 MiB and 3 passes, on Node v24.21.0 where `crypto.argon2` is present [P]); if a later change of the plan's Node version loses `crypto.argon2` the fallback is **scrypt** (N = 2^17, r = 8, p = 1, with `maxmem` set to **256 MiB**: Node's default is 32 MiB, and a value of exactly 128 MiB is refused as well, because the cost is a little more than 128 MiB [W]). At most 2 hashes run at the same time, and the throttle of A13 limits how many logins may wait for them. A wrong e-mail and a wrong password give the same answer, `JV_BAD_LOGIN` ("Wrong email or password."), in about the same time (an e-mail that does not exist still runs one dummy hash). A correct password for a user who is not active gets `403 JV_FORBIDDEN` with `details.reason = "inactive"`, which the screen shows as "Your account is not active". The login screen shows no credentials or hints. The prototype's hard-coded `admin/admin123` and `staff/staff123` (`authStore.ts:13-16`) are deleted from the code and from the build. *Test:* T-D12, T-D25, T-E01. *Source:* SEC-01; docs #10, #14 [N]; NODE-DESIGN 2.4, 2.5.
- **U2.** There is no sign-up: no route creates an account except the admin door (U3), and a request to a sign-up-looking path answers `404`. *Test:* T-D13. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.8.
- **U3.** Accounts are made only by Harsh, through the **admin door**: routes under `/admin/v1/` (create a user with a random temporary password that is shown once, reset a password, **change a user's role**, deactivate, activate, list). They work only while the environment variable `JV_ADMIN_TOKEN_HASH` is set; they compare the bearer token with that hash in constant time; they send no CORS headers and refuse any request that carries an `Origin` header, so a web page can never call them; they are throttled (A13); and every call is audited with the actor `admin-cli` (D16). On his Mac it is one command (`npm run admin -- add-user`) that asks for the token, the e-mail, the name and the role and prints the temporary password; no SQL and no database password is needed on his Mac. The first owner is made the same way, and **the admin door is the only way to make an owner**. Whether the token stays switched on after the first owner exists, and whether the owner may make other owners, is O41 (default: off until needed; only the admin door makes owners). A lost password is reset by Harsh the same way (a reset sets a new temporary password, sets `must_change_password` and **ends every session of that user**, so a stolen session does not survive it); there is no e-mail reset in M1 (outbound mail is unverified and adds attack surface). *Test:* T-D27, T-E09. *Source:* review Addendum A.4 (staff create and reset); NODE-DESIGN 2.8.
- **U4.** Sessions are **server-side**. A `sessions` row (D24) holds the SHA-256 of a 32-byte random ticket that the browser carries in a cookie (U12); every call looks it up, so a session ends at once when a user is deactivated, changes password or logs out. Two limits are enforced on the server on every call: an **absolute lifetime of 14 days** and an **idle lifetime of 12 hours** (`last_seen_at`); a session past either is ended and the answer is `401 JV_SESSION_EXPIRED`. The screen's polling does not count as activity (`GET /v1/sync/*` and `POST /v1/sync/poll` never move `last_seen_at`); every other call does, and so does `POST /v1/auth/activity`, which the screen sends on a click or key press at most once a minute. The screen also logs itself out after 12 hours without a click or key press (C16). The 12-hour and 14-day figures are choices for Siddhi (O42, which replaces O10b), not facts from any document; OWASP's usual idle range is minutes, not hours [N]. This gives on every plan what Supabase offered only on Pro. *Test:* T-D26, T-E08. *Source:* docs #5 of draft 3, F17; NODE-DESIGN 2.3, 2.7.
- **U5.** After login, and at start-up, the screen calls `GET /v1/auth/me`; the role it returns decides which routes and menu items exist (1.3). The API decides what is allowed (section 3). The screen calls `me` again whenever a call answers `403 JV_FORBIDDEN` for something it had been allowed to read (U6). *Test:* T-E01.
- **U6.** The screen has no login library. Its state changes only on login, logout, `me` and any `401` (`JV_UNAUTHENTICATED`, `JV_SESSION_EXPIRED`): a `401` from any call, a poll included, empties memory, stops the pollers and shows the login screen once, without a loop. A `403 JV_FORBIDDEN` from the poll is not shown as an error first: the screen pauses the poll and calls `me`; if the role has changed it empties memory (the owner-only data, `sku_private` and the audit data included), stops the pollers and opens the gate of U7 again with the new role; if the role is the same it shows the error once. At most one `me` call is made for each 403, so there is no loop. Every poll answer also carries the caller's current role (C4), so a change is seen within one poll even before a 403. *Test:* T-E01 (login and logout repeated 20 times without a hang; a user demoted from owner to biller while signed in loses `sku_private` and the audit data from memory within one poll, and the screen does not loop on 403). *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.9.
- **U7.** One module-level gate in the application layout shows, in order: a loading screen, the login screen, then the app. No page reads the session on its own: today `app/page.tsx:17-23` reads it in a mount effect that never updates after the layout's login, so the Owner sees no rate card right after logging in (AC2). Pages read the role from a `useSession` hook. *Test:* T-E01. *Source:* hazard 3, AC2.
- **U8.** Logout calls `POST /v1/auth/logout` (the session is ended and the cookie cleared), empties every in-memory cache and stops every poller. After logout no business data remains in memory or in browser storage. *Test:* T-E05. *Source:* SEC-08.
- **U9.** Passwords are at least 10 and at most 128 characters; a short built-in list of very common passwords, and any password that contains the e-mail's local part, are refused. There is no leaked-password lookup in M1 (it would send data to a third party; O29 is closed by this rule). A new user gets a temporary password from Harsh's command and must change it at the first login (U11). *Test:* T-D25. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.4.
- **U10.** The website's build (on Vercel, O39) contains the API's address (`NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL`) only. No secret, no key and no database connection string appears in the repository, the build output or the browser; the database credentials exist only in Hostinger's environment variables, Harsh's password manager and, during a migration or import window, his Mac (S1, A3), and the API refuses to start if one is missing. *Test:* T-D12, T-D29. *Source:* SEC-09; NODE-DESIGN 8 item 1.
- **U11.** *(new in draft 4)* **First login.** A user with `must_change_password` can call only change-password, logout and `me`; every other call answers `403 JV_PASSWORD_CHANGE_REQUIRED`. Changing the password verifies the current one, stores the new hash, ends every other session of that user and clears the flag. *Test:* T-D27. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.5, 2.8.
- **U12.** *(new in draft 4)* **The session cookie** is named `__Host-jv_session` (so it needs `Secure` and `Path=/` and has no `Domain`: it belongs to the API's host only), is `HttpOnly`, `Secure` and `SameSite=Strict`, and has `Max-Age` equal to the absolute lifetime. A new ticket is made at every login and the old session of that browser is ended (against session fixation). *Test:* T-D25. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 2.3; docs #14 [N].

### 4.2 Photos

- **P1.** Photos live **in MariaDB** (decision of Harsh, 15 Oct 2026), in `design_photos` (D26): one photo is a full image and its 320 px thumbnail under one id; **at most 3 photos per design**; 2 MiB limit per file; only JPEG, PNG and WebP, decided by the file's first bytes (JPEG `FF D8 FF`, PNG `89 50 4E 47`, WebP `RIFF`...`WEBP`) and not by the file name or the declared type alone. The numbers are kept in the settings key `photos` (D3). A photo can be read by anyone who knows its id (a version-4 UUID, unguessable) with no session, because the later public catalogue needs the same (O11, whose default stands); `NODE-DESIGN.md` 6.2 item 5 asked for a session on every photo read, and that is not adopted. `[NEEDS CLARIFICATION: should photos stay private until the catalogue is switched on? Siddhi, default: public with unguessable ids (O11)]` *Test:* T-D21. *Source:* docs #8, DAT-06; HOSTINGER-FACTS 3.
- **P2.** The photo is made in the browser: the screen resizes to a longest side of **1000 px at JPEG quality 72** and makes the 320 px thumbnail (Harsh's decision of 15 Oct 2026; draft 3 had 1200 px at 0.82 as coded in `AddDesignClient.tsx:37-64`); the server does no image processing. The screen makes the design's id (a version-4 UUID) and one id per photo **before** uploading, uploads each file with `PUT /v1/designs/{design_id}/photos/{photo_id}?kind=full|thumb` (the raw bytes as the body), and then calls `create_design` (X8) or `PATCH /v1/designs/{id}` with the photo ids; X8 refuses a design id that already exists. The upload checks only that both ids are well-formed UUIDs and that fewer than 3 photos are already stored for that design id; it does **not** require the design to exist, because it usually does not yet, so `design_photos.design_id` has no foreign key (D26). The rows are **insert-only**: an upload of a (`photo_id`, `kind`) pair that already exists is refused with `JV_NOT_ALLOWED`, so nobody can overwrite a photo (the pair is the key, D26, so the thumbnail sent after the full image under the same id is accepted); a replacement is a new id. X8 and the design `PATCH` are the authoritative check: they accept a photo id only if (a) at most 3 ids are attached, (b) every id exists with **both** kinds stored, (c) its `design_id` is this design's id, (d) it is not removed, and, for a staff caller, (e) the old ids stay in their order and only new ones are added at the end (A5); anything else is `JV_INVALID_INPUT`. Only validated UUIDs name a photo; a client cannot choose a path. *Test:* T-D21, T-C03. *Source:* F16; NODE-DESIGN 6.2.
- **P3.** Roles: the owner and staff may upload a **new** photo; only the owner may replace or remove one (a replacement is a new photo and the removal of the old one); a biller cannot upload or remove. A photo is read with `GET /v1/photos/{photo_id}/full` or `.../thumb` by anyone who has the id, with `Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable`, an `ETag`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: cross-origin` (the security-header plugin's default would make the browser refuse the website's `<img>` [U]; an id never changes content, so the browser may keep it for good); a removed photo answers `404`, but a browser or a shared cache that already holds it keeps serving it, for up to a year, because the headers say `immutable`; catalogue photos are not secret, so this is accepted, and putting a version into the photo address instead is Siddhi's choice (O11). A staff `PATCH` can add photo ids to a design and never remove one (A5). Removing a photo is a tombstone: `removed_at` is set and `bytes` is emptied by an `UPDATE` (S7), which frees the space for new photos, and the owner's removal is audited. A file over 2 MiB is refused with `413 JV_PAYLOAD_TOO_LARGE` and a non-image with `415 JV_UNSUPPORTED_MEDIA_TYPE`. Uploads and reads are throttled (A13). *Test:* T-D21. *Source:* F16; NODE-DESIGN 6.2; RF4-29.
- **P4.** A design row holds only photo ids. A `CHECK` rejects any value that starts with `data:` (tested on a text column and on a JSON array with `JSON_SEARCH` [S]), the API refuses anything that is not a UUID, and the page never stores image text in a row. *Test:* T-F02. *Source:* hazard 6, `AddDesignClient.tsx:36-63`.
- **P5.** The photos are in the database, so they share the plan's 3 GB with the business data. The nightly export leaves the photo bytes out and a once-only photo copy copies each photo to the backup folder on the plan (B2; a photo never changes, so one copy is enough), which keeps the export small and keeps the second heartbeat of I12. The owner's dashboard shows the database size and goes red at 2 GB of the 3 GB limit (O46). The size is worked out as `DATA_LENGTH + INDEX_LENGTH` of the other tables (after an `ANALYZE TABLE` of each, which the user may run [W]) plus `SUM(byte_size)` of the live photos plus 12 percent for InnoDB overhead, because `information_schema` alone lags badly for a table of few large rows: it said 0.0 MB after 65 MB of photos were inserted and 69.5 MB after `ANALYZE TABLE` [W]. Measured on four of Siddhi's photos resized as the screen does, one photo with its thumbnail takes about 250 KB in InnoDB at 1200 px and about 180 KB at the 1000 px, quality 72 setting that Harsh chose; business data at the volumes of C2 takes about 73 MB, 72 percent of it the audit log. At the chosen setting 3 photos on each of 2,000 designs come to about 1.1 GB, and the database stays under the red line up to about 3,300 designs [S: a calculation from four measured photos on a local database, not a measurement on Hostinger]. *Test:* T-F10, T-R03. *Source:* HOSTINGER-FACTS 3; NODE-DESIGN 6.3.
- **P6.** A photo that is uploaded but never attached to a design (the design was not created, or the screen stopped before the `PATCH`) is harmless; a monthly sweep lists, for the owner, the photos that no design refers to, and empties their bytes once the owner agrees (an `UPDATE`, S7). *Test:* none in M1.
- **P7.** *(new in draft 4)* All photo code goes through one `PhotoStore` interface (put, get, remove, and sizes) with one implementation, the table of D26. A second implementation on an outside bucket can replace it without touching the screens or the routes if Siddhi's catalogue turns out larger than the budget of P5 (O47). *Test:* T-F10. *Source:* decision of Harsh, 15 Oct 2026; HOSTINGER-FACTS 3.

### 4.3 Audit, numbers and repeat-safety

- **L1.** Every insert and update on `users`, `settings`, `daily_rates`, `categories`, `subcategories`, `designs`, `skus`, `sku_private`, `customers`, `bills`, `bill_items`, `receipts` and `receipt_allocations` produces **exactly one** `audit_log` row (never two), in the same transaction as the change, with the session user, the role and the old and new row as JSON; an update that changes only `updated_at` produces none (D23); columns that must never be copied (`password_hash`, `token_hash`) are on an exclude list kept per table in one file, and T-D14 checks table by table that no audit row holds a hash, because the owner reads this table and it goes into the backups. Logins, password changes, admin-door calls and job runs are written as `event` rows (D16); failed logins are not written one by one but as one summary row for each hour (D24). **Mechanism (the proof of O48 showed that `CREATE TRIGGER` works for the plan's database user and fires [P], so (a) is the default):** (a) generated triggers write the rows, reading the actor from a session variable that the transaction wrapper sets at the start of each transaction and clears at the end (the variable lives on the pooled connection and survives a commit [S]); then a change typed by hand in phpMyAdmin and the import are recorded too, with no actor and the role `sql` or `import`; (b) if the full trigger set cannot be built (the actor variable and the JSON of old and new rows are not proven on the plan yet, T-D14), the API writes the same rows in the same transaction, and a change typed by hand is then not audited (O50); **CMP-09 (section 14) is closed for everything the app does and, with (a), for a hand edit in phpMyAdmin too.** Never both for one table. Draft 3 used triggers; draft 4 does not depend on them. Every service function and every edit has a test that counts the changed rows and the audit rows and compares them, run against **both** mechanisms, whichever is in use (T-D14). *Test:* T-D14. *Source:* CMP-09; NODE-DESIGN 8 item 4, D-Q2; PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md D16.
- **L2.** No route writes, updates or deletes `audit_log`, not even the owner's, and the code contains no statement that does (the statement wrapper and a grep test, as for S7). Where triggers work (L1 (a)), `BEFORE UPDATE` and `BEFORE DELETE` triggers with `SIGNAL` on `audit_log` make it append-only for every user, the database owner included [S]; without them an `UPDATE` typed by hand could still change it (Delete and Drop are blocked by S7 either way). *Test:* T-D14.
- **L3.** Numbers come from the `sys_counters` row of the series. **The row exists before the transaction that needs it.** The function works the series out once (the year from the API clock, S5) and uses that one value in both steps. First it reads `SELECT 1 FROM sys_counters WHERE series = ?` (no lock at READ COMMITTED); only when the row is missing it runs `INSERT IGNORE INTO sys_counters (series, last_value) VALUES (?, 0)`, on a checked-out connection and inside the error map of S8, so that a lock wait there is `JV_BUSY` and not a raw 1205 (the earlier `INSERT ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE series = series` took the row lock and waited behind the bill before it; a review run saw it fail after the full lock wait). Then, in the **same transaction** as the row it numbers, the number is taken with `UPDATE sys_counters SET last_value = LAST_INSERT_ID(last_value + 1) WHERE series = ?` and **read from the OK packet of that `UPDATE` (`insertId` in mysql2); no statement runs between the two, and `SELECT LAST_INSERT_ID()` is never used**, because any `INSERT` into an `AUTO_INCREMENT` table after the update (the audit log is one, L1) changes what it returns (a review run read an audit row's id, 86,857, after the real number 43). An `UPDATE` that changes no row means the series row is missing: a bug, answered `JV_INTERNAL`. The update takes a row lock, so two callers are serialised; a rolled-back transaction rolls the counter back, so there are no gaps and no repeats (an `AUTO_INCREMENT` leaves a gap after a rollback and is never used for a human number [S]). The first step matters: when a new series is created inside the transaction and its first user rolls back while two others wait, one of the two gets a deadlock (1213) [S, spike F3]; a series row that exists beforehand avoids it, which counts on 1 January for `bill:<PREFIX>:<YYYY>` and on first use of `opening` and `design:<CATEGORY PREFIX>` (`sku:<design_no>` is made inside X8, where nobody else can know the new design number). A number that already exists (an imported code, IM3) is skipped by looking ahead with `SELECT 1 FROM bills WHERE bill_no = ?` (no error and no lock) and taking the next number, at most 100 times, then `JV_INVALID_INPUT`. A duplicate-key error on a bill's number is never caught and answers `JV_INTERNAL`: catching it would keep the failed insert's next-key locks until the commit (a review run saw a neighbour wait the full lock timeout) and S12 (1) forbids carrying on after a database error. Series: `bill:<PREFIX>:<YYYY>`, `opening`, `design:<CATEGORY PREFIX>`, `sku:<design_no>`. Measured locally: gap-free under 25 percent injected failures and 5 percent killed connections, and 650 to 750 numbers a second through the one counter row; not measured over a network [S]. *Test:* T-C01, T-C16. *Source:* docs #7, BIL-01, INV-09, hazard 4; SPIKE (f) and section 8 (F3); RM4-5, RM4-6, RM4-7.
- **L4.** Formats as coded: a bill is `<prefix>-<YYYY>-<4 digits>` (`appStore.ts:154-159`, prefix from `settings.estimate.prefix`, default `JV`; after 9,999 the digits simply grow); a design is `<category prefix>-<5 digits>` (`designPadding`); a piece is `<design_no>-<3 digits>` (`skuPadding`); a barcode is `<barcodePrefix>-<sku_no>` (default `BC`). The year is the **Asia/Kolkata year when the bill is saved**, so a backdated bill still takes the current year's number; whether numbering should follow the financial year is a question for the CA (O31). Opening-balance bills are `OB-<4 digits>`. A number is never reused, even after archiving. The screens do **not** show a "next number" before saving (the prototype did, `BillingPOSClient.tsx:70`, `designStore.ts:176`); they show "Number assigned when saved". *Test:* T-C01, T-C03, T-F07. *Source:* AC6, F18.
- **L5.** Repeat-safety: every function that creates something takes the request header `Idempotency-Key` (a UUID; draft 3's `p_request_id`), generated once per function call on the screen (a new-customer flow calls X6 and then X1, each with its own key; a retry of one call reuses that call's key). Its **first statement** is `INSERT INTO sys_idempotency (request_id, fn, actor, args_hash, claim_token) ... ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE request_id = request_id`, where `args_hash` is a SHA-256 of a canonical JSON of the route name, the path ids, the body and the actor id with keys sorted, and `claim_token` is a fresh random value. The function then reads that row back `FOR UPDATE`: if the token is its own it is first and carries on, storing its result in that row **in the same transaction**; with mysql2's default flags `affectedRows` is 1 for a fresh insert and for a duplicate alike (docs #7), so the token, not the count, tells which. If the token is another request's (the insert first waits for any in-flight identical request to commit), the function returns the stored result with the same status and the header `Idempotent-Replay: true` when `fn`, `actor` and `args_hash` all match, and raises `JV_INVALID_INPUT` otherwise (the same key with a different cart is refused, not answered with the first result). A first request that rolls back leaves no row, so the waiting twin becomes first and runs. A double click, a retry after a dropped connection, a second tab or two identical requests in flight at once cannot save twice and never show a false error. A request that fails the route wrapper's checks (session, must-change-password gate, matrix row, body validation, A6) never reaches the claim and so never uses up its key; the function's own role check (X0 item 2) comes after the claim, and when it refuses, the rollback removes the claim row again. A writing call without an `Idempotency-Key` is refused with `JV_INVALID_INPUT` (X7, the ordinary edits of A5 and the download record of B3 need none). The database runs READ-COMMITTED (S11), because under REPEATABLE READ an old snapshot made the duplicate probe fail with error 1020 [S]. Measured locally: 8 identical requests at once gave one bill, one counter step and 7 replays; different keys do not wait for each other [S]. *Test:* T-C05, T-C14, T-C18, T-E06. *Source:* hazard 1, BIL-02, F5; SPIKE (c); NODE-DESIGN 4.6.
- **L6.** For rows of `sys_idempotency` older than 90 days the nightly job (I8) empties `result`; the row itself stays (about 150 bytes; S7 allows no delete). A repeat of a key older than 90 days is refused with `JV_INVALID_INPUT` ("this request is too old to repeat"); it is never run again. *Test:* T-C05.

## 5. The cache contract (how the screens talk to the API)

The six store modules (`appStore`, `designStore`, `orderStore`, `settingsStore`, `catalogStore`, `authStore`; 1,408 lines, imported by 15, 17, 1, 6, 6 and 11 component files) keep their exported names. Behind them, memory is filled from the API after login and kept fresh by polling. This is what lets about 15 files that write data change, instead of all 37. The prototype's prices, totals and ledger are not computed again in the stores; the saved figures come back from the API.

- **C1.** Reads stay synchronous from memory (`getDesigns()`, `getInvoices()`, `getCustomers()`, `getSettings()` ...). `getInvoices()` returns bills in the prototype's `Invoice` shape (camelCase, with `items`, `payments`, and the derived `amountReceived`, `pendingAmount`, `status`, `fineWeightPending`) so the screens that only read need no change. *Test:* T-E04.
- **C2.** A **loading gate** in the application layout (U7) renders no data page until the first full hydrate has finished for the signed-in role. It shows progress ("3 of 8 tables"). If hydrate fails it shows an error with a retry button. It never falls back to sample data or to an empty list. **Time budget:** for the M1 volumes (up to 5,000 pieces, 2,000 customers, 3,000 bills) the first hydrate finishes within 10 seconds on a typical 4G connection. The payload is estimated at 8 to 12 MB before compression and whether Hostinger's proxy compresses is unknown [U]; the budget is therefore a test on the staging app on Hostinger, not a promise (O48). *Test:* T-E01, T-E05, T-F03. *Source:* DAT-05, hazard 8, F3.
- **C3.** Hydrate and poll are the same call: `GET /v1/sync/{resource}?updated_after=&after_key=&limit=`, ordered by (`updated_at`, key) and **keyset-paged**, with `limit` at most 1,000 (a constant in the API, so there is no silent cut by the server). The reply is `{rows, next}`: `next` is present when the page was full and the client calls again with `updated_after = next.updated_at` and `after_key = next.key`; `next: null` ends the load. A hydrate is a poll that starts from the beginning. Every answer carries `server_time` (the API's clock, S5), and **the first poll after a hydrate starts from the `server_time` of the hydrate's FIRST page minus the 30-second overlap, not from the stamp of the last row**: a row that was stamped but not yet committed when its page was read (a transaction lasts up to 20 seconds, S12) would otherwise be lost for good when the load took longer than the overlap (a simulated case on MariaDB 11.8.9: a row stamped 10:00:00 and committed 4 seconds after page 1 was read was missing from the first poll after a slow load, RF4-5). A row changed during a hydrate moves to the end of the order and is received again, and the cache merges by id keeping the greater `updated_at`, so no row committed during or after a load is skipped. The key is `id` (`setting_key` for `settings`, `sku_id` for `sku_private`; `customer_balances` is keyed by the customer's id). The comparison is written `updated_at > ? OR (updated_at = ? AND key > ?)`, because whether MariaDB turns the row-value form `(a, b) > (x, y)` into an index range is unknown [U]; a test reads `EXPLAIN` for it (A7). **The key is compared as its own type (a UUID as a UUID, never as text), and no `SELECT` gives a column the name of another column that `ORDER BY` uses**: the database session's export skipped rows with random version-4 ids when a column cast to text was given the key's own name, and the pages of a poll have the same shape (`BACKUP-RESTORE.md`). Each page is one `SELECT` with an explicit column list for the caller's role. The role decides the set. **Owner:** all resources **except** `audit_log`, `check_runs` and `backup_runs`, which are read on demand and paged when the owner opens them. **Biller:** stock (without `sku_private`), customers, `bills` (the header: subtotal, discount, additional charges, round-off and notes, which the `Invoice` shape needs and `bill_summary` does not carry), `bill_summary`, `bill_items`, `receipts`, `receipt_allocations`, `customer_balances`, rates, settings, categories, users. **Staff:** stock, categories, the seven settings keys of A4, users. The ledger view `customer_ledger` is loaded **per customer when a customer card opens** (`GET /v1/customers/{id}/ledger`, keyset-paged by the order key of V3) and reloaded when that customer's `updated_at` changes; `daily_totals` is loaded for a date range when a report opens (`GET /v1/daily-totals?from=&to=`, at most 400 days for the owner; a biller gets today and yesterday only, A4, O25). The owner's other reads are `GET /v1/audit-log` (newest first, at most 200 a page), `GET /v1/health/checks` and `GET /v1/dashboard/health` (the dashboard line of section 12 in one call). *Test:* T-F03 (2,500 pieces all load), T-E07. *Source:* F3; NODE-DESIGN 4.2; RF4-5.
- **C4.** **Polling** (Realtime is not used). Each resource group keeps a **cursor**: the greatest (`updated_at`, key) pair it has fully received, taken from the data, never from the browser's clock. Every 8 seconds (a setting, 5 to 10), and at once after any write the screen made, one request `POST /v1/sync/poll` carries a cursor for each resource the role may read (if the role may not read one of them the whole call is `403 JV_FORBIDDEN`, with no partial answer, and the screen answers it as U6 says; it does not move the session's idle clock, U4); each resource answers with rows after its cursor minus a **30-second overlap**, ordered by (`updated_at`, key) and **keyset-paged** in pages of 1,000. **All resources of one poll are read inside one read-only snapshot** (`SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ`, then `START TRANSACTION READ ONLY, WITH CONSISTENT SNAPSHOT`, both allowed to the plan's user [W], committed at the end of the poll), so a poll never shows a bill without its lines or a payment without its allocation; at READ COMMITTED each statement would see its own moment (docs #5). The answer also carries `server_time` and the caller's current `role`. The cursor moves **only after every page of that poll has arrived**. Results are merged by `id`, keeping the row with the greater `updated_at`, compared as microsecond numbers parsed from the exact text. `bill_summary` and `customer_balances` expose the base row's `updated_at`, which the touches move whenever a payment or allocation changes (D23), so pending and balance figures refresh. A service function cannot hold a row longer than the overlap: it takes its contended locks (customer, pieces, the number counter last) before it stamps any row, the lock wait is 5 seconds, and the whole call has a 12-second deadline with an 8-second statement limit (S11, S12), so no row is stamped more than 20 seconds before its commit. **Bulk work outside the API (an import, a repair script) is not bounded by that**, so it ends as IM1 says: one statement that sets `updated_at` to the current time on every row it changed, just before `COMMIT`, each transaction under 20 seconds, and then a new value of the `settings` row `data_epoch`; a screen that sees `data_epoch` change hydrates again from the start. The server clock and the API's clock are assumed to agree within a few seconds (a hand edit in phpMyAdmin is stamped by the database clock, the API's rows by the API clock, S6). A hidden tab polls every 30 seconds and at once when it becomes visible. Nothing is deleted and archived rows stay readable (A4), so no row ever disappears from a poll. *Test:* T-E02, T-E07, T-C15. *Source:* docs #1, #5, F3, RF-2; NODE-DESIGN 4.2.
- **C5.** Two kinds of write:
  - **(a) Ordinary edits** (stock content columns, categories, customer content columns, settings, daily rates): memory is updated at once and the row is marked *pending*; one write is awaited with **only the changed fields** and the stale check of A5 (the exact `updated_at` text); a pending row ignores polled copies until the write finishes; on success the returned row replaces it, on failure memory is rolled back and the reason shown.
  - **(b) Functions** (every function of section 10 and the opening-balance function): **never optimistic.** The screen shows a saving state, calls the function with a fresh `Idempotency-Key`, applies the **rows the function returns**, and on failure shows the message for the error code. A piece never shows Sold, and a bill never shows saved, before the API says so. *Test:* T-C06, T-E06. *Source:* hazard 1, `BillingSummaryPanel.tsx:163-167`.
- **C6.** The Save button is disabled while a save is running. A retry of the same user action reuses the same `Idempotency-Key` (L5). *Test:* T-E06.
- **C7.** **Offline.** When a poll or a write fails because the network is unreachable, a banner says "Offline: changes cannot be saved", every Save control is disabled, reads continue from memory, and polling retries with back-off (8, 16, then 30 seconds). When the API answers with a gateway error or a reply that is not JSON (an idle app being started, H7), the banner says "Waking up…" instead, and the call is retried with back-off for up to 30 seconds (a money call with the same `Idempotency-Key`). One successful call clears the banner. *Test:* T-E03.
- **C8.** **Browser storage.** No business data is written to `localStorage`, `sessionStorage` or IndexedDB. The only thing the browser holds from the login is the session cookie, which JavaScript cannot read (U12). The old `jaishri.*` keys are never read or written by the new build (the opening-data import reads them once from the old build, IM7). *Test:* T-E05. *Source:* DAT-05, DAT-07, SEC-08.
- **C9.** Render-time reads and `useState(getX())` stay valid because data is in memory after the gate; the 16 files that re-read the stores every second keep doing so only against memory, and none of them calls the network. *Test:* T-E02. *Source:* hazard 8.
- **C10.** The static export means no page depends on server rendering: every page is a client component behind the gate and no page touches `window` during prerender (the prototype's `typeof window` guards stay). The prototype already builds as 16 static routes (docs #17). *Test:* T-D12 (the build produces every route).
- **C11.** The cache never makes up a number or an id, except a new design's id (P2); new rows, ids and numbers come back from the database. *Test:* T-C01. *Source:* hazard 4.
- **C12.** **One pricing file, two copies of that file.** The live total on the billing screen comes from `src/lib/pricing.ts`, an integer implementation of the pricing (money in paise, weight in milligrams; no floating point). The API runs the **same file, byte for byte**, as `src/domain/pricing.ts`, next to a file `PRICING-SOURCE.txt` that names the source commit and the SHA-256; a script `check:pricing` compares the two hashes, and the app's pricing unit tests also run against the API's copy. The source of truth stays in the app repository. Because the website and the API are deployed separately, the API returns the SHA-256 of its `pricing.ts` in `GET /v1/auth/me` (`pricing_sha256`), the website's build knows its own hash, and on a mismatch the screen shows a banner and disables Save on bills. The saved bill always shows the API's figures; if they differ from the screen's preview the screen shows the saved figures and a notice. `quote_bill` (X7) runs the pricing without saving, so a test can compare it with the browser's preview on random input. This is a stronger guarantee than draft 3's database-against-browser parity test, because the money is priced by the code the screen previews with; the independent check is an exact-decimal oracle (a BigInt oracle and the SQL `DECIMAL` version of the spike, which agreed on 2,546 random bills and 20,000 half-way cases [S]). *Test:* T-A14, T-A15. *Source:* hazard 5; NODE-DESIGN 1.4; SPIKE (b).
- **C13.** Every error code (S8) has a plain message; a refusal that happens before any write (for example `JV_OVERPAYMENT`, `JV_PIECE_UNAVAILABLE`, `JV_INVALID_INPUT`) says that nothing was changed; an unknown error (`JV_INTERNAL`) and a `JV_BUSY` that fails again after the one automatic retry show "Could not confirm that this was saved." and the screen at once re-reads the customer's bills and receipts (C17), so the user sees whether the bill exists before keying it again (S8: a connection lost during `COMMIT` leaves it unknown); the detail goes to the console only. *Test:* T-E04 (the error map), T-C19 and each function test.
- **C14.** Reports, dashboard figures and the customer card are computed from the **same selectors** over the derived views (section 11) and business dates (S5), so they cannot disagree. **Opening-balance bills are not sales**: they are excluded from sales figures and from the daily cash totals. *Test:* T-B15, T-B21. *Source:* LED-04, LED-07, LED-08, LED-10, F4.
- **C15.** In M1 these are not connected and not reachable: Order Book, the public catalogue and its admin screens, the sample Audit and Sales tabs, the Estimate screen's date editor and edit mode, and the Reports screen's date controls and export buttons (QA LED-09, O32). *Test:* T-E04. *Source:* reviewer, `PLAN.md` P3-T3.
- **C16.** The screen logs out by itself after 12 hours without a click or key press (U4). *Test:* T-E08.
- **C17.** After any write that touches money, the screen re-reads that customer's `bill_summary` rows and `customer_balances` row at once (the next poll would also bring them). *Test:* T-E02.
- **C18.** The API looks up the session and the user on every call (A10), so a user who is deactivated, or whose role changed, in the middle of a shift gets a `401` or the new role's limits at the next call, at most one poll interval later; a deactivated user is logged out and the memory cleared (U6), and a changed role arrives as the `role` of the next poll answer (C4) or as a `403`, after which the screen calls `me`, clears the owner-only data if the role is lower and opens the gate of U7 again (U6). Draft 3 read the user's own `profiles` row in every poll for this; the check now belongs to the API. *Test:* T-E01, T-D26. *Source:* RF-12.

## 6. Backups, restore, hosting, build and import

### 6.1 Backups and restore

- **B1.** The production database is the MariaDB database on Harsh's Hostinger Business plan; no database plan is bought. Hostinger makes a daily automatic backup of files and databases (Business and higher), which is a bonus and **not the plan**: its retention is not documented, Hostinger's backups page lists "Database only", "Files only" and "Full backup" as restore options [V], and a restore overwrites the current files and database [V]. Point-in-time recovery does not exist here, so up to 24 hours of entries can be lost in a disaster (O28). *Test:* none: a plan step, ticked in the go or no-go checklist (plan task P3-T8); the nightly dump (B2) is the plan. *Source:* docs #12, #13.
- **B2.** Every night two things are written to a **backup folder on Harsh's Hostinger plan** (Harsh's decision of 15 Oct 2026: no off-site store and no new subscription, O45): `~/domains/<site>/jv-backup/`, in the site's home folder, outside the build folder and outside the web root, which survives a redeploy while the build folder does not [P]. The two things are: (1) a **full export of the data made by the API's own Node code, without the photo `bytes`**, encrypted; and (2) a **photo copy**: each photo object (`full` and `thumb`) that has not been copied yet is written once and recorded in `sys_photo_copies` (D25). A photo never changes (its id is its content, P3), so one copy is enough; an export that held 2 GB of photos, kept as 30 daily and 12 monthly copies, would be about 100 GB, far above the plan's 50 GB of disk [H], and would make every restore drill heavy (`NODE-DESIGN.md` D-Q11, which reverses the database session's note that the dump holds the photos). **The export:** the job reads every table in keyset pages inside one read-only snapshot (`SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ`, then `START TRANSACTION READ ONLY, WITH CONSISTENT SNAPSHOT`, set on a connection of the pool for that job, docs #5, [W]) and writes newline-delimited JSON (DECIMAL as text, the `bytes` column left out) **to a temporary file first (in `/tmp` or in `jv-tmp/` next to `jv-backup/`, never in the build folder); it then closes the snapshot, and only then gzips and encrypts**, so the snapshot never sits idle against a connection cut. It leaves out `sessions`, `sys_login_attempts` and expired `sys_idempotency` rows, and it holds **no table definitions**: `SHOW CREATE TABLE` leaves out triggers, so the migration files in the repository are the schema (B4). Encryption: a fresh AES-256-GCM key per file, wrapped with an RSA public key held in an environment variable; **the private key is held by Harsh and by the owner separately and never on the server or in the repository** (O40). The photo copy is **not encrypted** (product photos meant for a later catalogue, O11; the export is where customer data lives). **Layout and the create-once rule:** `daily/<date>`, `monthly/<yyyy-mm>` (the first run of each month also writes one) and `photos/<photo id>-<kind>`. A file is written under a temporary name in the same folder and **linked into place**, so a name is created once: the same bytes again are accepted (a retry after a half-finished run) and other bytes under the same name are refused; the size is checked with `stat` (the database session's `directoryStore` and `directoryPhotos`, tested in commit `6ec5636`). The job deletes `daily/` files older than 30 days and `monthly/` files older than 400 days, and never anything in `photos/`; it writes the folder's size into `backup_runs.detail`. **What this protects and what it does not, said plainly (Harsh accepted it on 15 Oct 2026; Siddhi is to be told, O45):** it protects against a bad deploy, a damaged or wrongly edited database, a lost table and a failed migration, by a restore from the export. It does **not** protect against the loss, suspension or expiry of the Hostinger account: the database, every export, the photo copy and Hostinger's own daily account backup (retention not stated, a restore overwrites live data [H][V]) are all in one account at one company, and the plan ends on **22 March 2027** (O46). And there is **no lock**: the job's account can delete files in the folder, so an attacker who controls the app can delete the backups as well; the audit-log triggers and Hostinger's separate daily backup are then the only protection. The one copy outside Hostinger is the owner's monthly **Download everything** (B3), which is why B3 is a requirement with a reminder, a health flag and a test. **Phases and slices:** the work runs in the phases `nightly_check`, `db_dump`, `photo_copy` and `housekeeping` of I8, in slices of about 40 seconds that record where they stopped; the next hourly tick goes on, and a `backup_runs` row with `ok` true is written only when a phase is complete for the day (D17). A year-5 export (about 400 MB, mostly audit log) is an estimate [N]; if the export does not fit in slices, the fallback is the same `exportDatabase` run from Harsh's Mac through Remote MySQL with a folder sink (O49), which also leaves a copy outside Hostinger; the earlier idea of a script run by hPanel's Node.js cron type is dropped, because a job of that type cannot be listed or deleted in hPanel [P]. A restore tool on Harsh's Mac rebuilds an empty database from the export and the photo copy (B4); the restore drill uses the same tool. `mariadb-dump` on Hostinger is unconfirmed (section 0 (d)); a Node export works without it. What the database does not hold is re-created from the runbook (B5): the unticked rights of A3, the hPanel cron job, the environment variables, the domain connections and the GitHub link. **Where it runs:** inside the hourly tick of I8, with connections from the pool (S11). **Visibility:** the dashboard shows the age of the last good export and of the last good photo copy and turns **red after 26 hours** (I12); the outside check of I8 fails the same way. Recovery point objective 24 hours (O28). *Test:* T-R01, T-R03, T-R04. *Source:* F15, RF-5; PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md B2; NODE-DESIGN 7.3 (second round and the amendment of 15 Oct evening), D-Q11; `BACKUP-RESTORE.md`; RF4-3, RF4-6, RF4-7, RF4-8.
- **B3.** **Download everything: a monthly duty of the owner, and the only copy outside Hostinger.** The owner has a "Download everything" button that builds, in the browser from the cache (after a fresh poll), one zip of CSV files: customers, bills, bill items, receipts, allocations, stock (designs and pieces), daily rates, categories and settings, and the users' names and roles (never a hash), plus `MANIFEST.csv` with the date and the row count of each file. The owner-only weight is included only for the owner. A text cell that starts with `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, a tab or a carriage return is written with a single quote in front (numbers are not touched), so a typed customer note cannot run as a formula when the owner opens the file in Excel (QA SEC-07). The zip is a **readable copy of the books** that the owner and her accountant can open if Hostinger is gone; it is not a database restore and holds no photos and no audit log. **The record:** when the zip has been built the screen sends its date and the counts to `POST /v1/owner/download-record` (owner only). The API accepts them only if every count equals the database's count of that table, give or take the rows made in the last two minutes (the cache is up to one poll behind); it then stores `{at, by, counts}` in the settings key `last_download` (L1 audits that write like any settings change, so there is exactly one audit row); counts that do not match answer `JV_INVALID_INPUT` and change nothing. **Reminder and flag:** the owner's dashboard shows a "Download everything" card with the date of the last download; from **28 days** after it (or after the first night the system ran, for the first download) the card turns amber and says it is time; the owner's health line (I12) turns **red when the last download is older than 35 days** (the default, O53). The flag lives in the health query (`readHealth`, I12), which reads `settings.last_download` and the oldest `check_runs` row; no new table is needed. The owner keeps each zip outside Hostinger (her own computer and a USB stick). *Test:* T-R05, T-E04. *Source:* DAT-07, RF-13; Harsh, 15 Oct 2026 (late evening).
- **B4.** **Restore, and the restore drill.** The restore tool (on Harsh's Mac; the drill uses it too) works in this order. (1) Apply the migration files to an empty database **without the trigger files** (triggers live in files of their own, O50), as the restricted user of A3, so that `schema_migrations` agrees with the repository. (2) Load the export's data **in foreign-key order**, parents before children, the order derived from the foreign keys of the migrations and not written by hand. (3) For every live `design_photos` row fetch the photo from `photos/` by id and kind, check its size and sha256, and **insert the row together with its bytes in one statement**: the export has no bytes and the `CHECK` of D26 refuses a live row without them (error 4025 [W]). A photo that is missing or does not match is **listed in the report, and its row is inserted as a tombstone** (`removed_at` = the restore time) so that the `CHECK` holds and nothing is skipped silently; the owner decides about each one. (4) Tombstones are restored as they are. (5) Apply the trigger files last, so that loading writes no audit rows for restored data. (6) Run the nightly check, compare the row count of every table with the manifest in the export, and print the report. The tool reads the backup folder over SFTP or SSH with Harsh's own login (SSH is active on the plan [H]) and uses the private key (B2, O40); no secret of the app is used for a restore, and the tool opens its database connections paced at no more than 10 in 1.2 seconds (S11). **The drill:** the latest export and the photo copy are restored into a throw-away MariaDB 11.8 in Docker on Harsh's Mac (the same version as Hostinger's 11.8.9; it costs nothing and leaves no cloud project to drop), and the whole takes **under 2 hours** with: the row count of every table equal to the source; the photo rows and their `sha256` values equal to the copied photos; 20 random photos opening; the runbook's settings list ticked (the unticked rights of A3, the cron job, the names of the environment variables, the domain connections); the nightly check passing against the restored copy; and the backup folder unreachable over HTTP. **Each key holder proves at drill 1 that they can decrypt**, by decrypting a small test file with their own copy of the private key (a key printed on paper is too long to type back, so the copy is a file kept in the holder's password manager; a shorter key format is O40's question). Drill 1 before the client demo, drill 2 before go-live, drill 3 seven days after go-live. *Test:* T-R02. *Source:* QA theme 11, `PLAN.md` P1-T9, RF-5; NODE-DESIGN 7.3; RF4-7.
- **B5.** A 2-page runbook (restore, add or deactivate a user with the admin command, reset a password, change a rate, deploy and roll back by a revert commit, rotate a secret, "the host is down", who to call) ships with training. It is accepted when someone who did not write it follows it to restore the throw-away copy. Rotating a secret (the admin token, the job token, the database password, the backup key pair) is an environment change and therefore a redeploy, so it is done at closing time (H7); **rotating the job token also means updating the caller side** (the command of the hPanel Custom cron job and the GitHub Actions secret), or the hourly tick stops in silence (I8); after saving a cron job the runbook checks that it appears in hPanel's list, because a job that is not listed cannot be removed (I8). Every deploy or "Apply changes" starts the app twice about 10 seconds apart [P], so nothing is tested for 30 seconds afterwards (H7). The owner's monthly Download everything (B3) is a runbook step too. The runbook also holds three more steps: switching Remote MySQL off again after a migration or import window (S1); how to fix a row by hand (S6: in the SQL tab, with an `UPDATE` that leaves `updated_at` out, or ending with `UPDATE ... SET updated_at = NOW(6)` and a new `data_epoch`); and the one purge planned for (S7): if the housekeeping tables ever fill the plan (I12), Harsh ticks Delete for the minutes it takes, removes the rows that the nightly job has already blanked, and unticks Delete again. *Test:* T-R02. *Source:* `PLAN.md` P3-T7; NODE-DESIGN 8 items 17, 18.
- **B6.** Hostinger's own backups belong to the plan and probably disappear with the plan or the account (no page says so [U]); their retention is not stated [V]. **All the copies the system makes itself (the database, the export, the photo copy and Hostinger's daily backup) live in one account at one company**; the only copy that can survive the loss, suspension or expiry of that account (the plan ends on 22 March 2027, O46) is the owner's monthly Download everything (B3), which is why B3 has a reminder, a health flag and a test. Siddhi is to be told this in plain words (O45). The production database and the plan are never deleted without Harsh and the owner both agreeing in writing. Two-step login is on for GitHub, Hostinger, Vercel, the domain's registrar or DNS account (O38) and the password manager before go-live, because a push to the connected branch deploys to production and because whoever controls the Hostinger account controls the data and every backup in it. *Test:* none: a rule about people, ticked in the go or no-go checklist.

### 6.2 Hosting and build

- **H1.** The site is a static export (`output: 'export'`) of Next.js 16, hosted on Vercel (live; Harsh's decision, O39). The security headers are set in the host's own configuration, because Next.js 16 lists `headers` in `next.config` as unsupported for a static export (docs #17): a `vercel.json` on Vercel (its `headers` entries apply to static files, docs #11), or a `_headers` file on Cloudflare Pages if the host ever moves (it applies to static assets, docs #15): `Strict-Transport-Security`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, a restrictive `Permissions-Policy`, and a `Content-Security-Policy` that allows only the site itself, the API's origin in `connect-src` **and `img-src`** (a policy that still named the Supabase origin would break every call and every photo), and the inline scripts the static export needs (introduced in report-only mode first). A static export can allow its inline scripts only with `'unsafe-inline'` (the Next.js 16.3 guide says nonces need dynamic rendering), so this policy does not stop an injected script; the session cookie is `HttpOnly`, so such a script cannot read it (U12), but it could still call the API as the signed-in user; the protection is React's escaping plus the build check of H3 that bans raw-HTML APIs. The API sets its own headers (A14). The terms risk of the Hobby plan is Harsh's, accepted by him (O39). *Test:* T-D12 (checked with `curl -I` on the host in use, Vercel today; a `_headers` file would be tested the same way before any move; the policy must name the API's origin in `connect-src` and `img-src` and no other outside origin). *Source:* SEC-09, docs #11, #15, #17.
- **H2.** **No source maps** in the build (`productionBrowserSourceMaps` off; no `.map` file in `out/`; confirmed on the P0-T2 build). *Test:* T-D12. *Source:* SEC-09.
- **H3.** A build check fails the build if `out/` contains `mysql://`, `mariadb://`, `DB_PASSWORD`, `DATABASE_URL`, `admin123`, `staff123`, or any `.map` file, or if `src/` uses `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` or `innerHTML`. (`admin123` and `staff123` are still in the P0-T2 build until U1 removes them.) A second check in the API repository (T-D29) scans every tracked file for URLs with passwords, `-----BEGIN`, key-shaped strings and the old `sb_secret_`, `admin123` and `staff123`. The draft 3 check of `api.max_rows` is gone: the page cap is a constant in the API with its own unit test (C3). *Test:* T-D12, T-D29. *Source:* U10, SEC-01.
- **H4.** Build-time environment on Vercel: `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL` only. It is public by design. *Test:* T-D12.
- **H5.** Three environments. **Local:** the tests run on a throw-away MariaDB 11.8 in Docker with its data in tmpfs, so no volume is left behind. **Staging:** a second Node app and a second database on the Hostinger plan (production and staging use 2 of the 5 Node app slots, and the failed test app and the proof app of O48 hold others until they are removed [H]; a Node app cannot be stopped, only removed [N]; the plan allows 150 or 300 databases depending on its version, O46 [V]), test data only, with its own website project and its own names (O38; until Siddhi's domain is connected, two `hostingersite.com` addresses, which are the same site [P]); the real-network checks run there (the C2 budget, the cold start, Safari cookies, restore drill 1). **Production:** the Hostinger app and database with real data. The same migration files go to all three (S9). Seeds differ: staging may hold test data, production holds only D20. The prototype's `.env` is never copied. Whether staging stays after go-live is O43. *Test:* T-F04. *Source:* PART-A-MARIADB-MAPPING.md H5; NODE-DESIGN 7.2, 9.2 DEC-7.
- **H6.** Next.js 16 needs a recent browser (Chrome 111 or newer). The devices at the counter are checked before go-live (O30). *Test:* none: a device check in the go or no-go checklist. *Source:* reviewer ask 5, docs #17.
- **H7.** *(new in draft 4)* **A stateless API that starts on demand.** Hostinger runs Node apps **on demand**: after a period without traffic the process is stopped and the next request starts it again, and a crashed app is restarted [V]; no page gives how long "a period" is; the proof app was **not stopped in any quiet gap up to 60 minutes** (one process for 4.5 hours), the first request after the 60-minute gap took 7.4 s (cause unknown), the overnight gaps were still being measured, and a deliberate crash was restarted in about a second [P] (O48). So the API keeps no state that matters in memory (sessions, login-attempt counts, repeat-safety rows, job state and the audit are in MariaDB; the other rate-limit counters may sit in memory and start again from zero after a restart, A13); the start-up check is quick (S11); the screen shows "Waking up…" and retries with back-off for up to 30 seconds when the front end answers with a gateway error or a reply that is not JSON, a money call retrying with the same Idempotency-Key (C7); the hourly tick probably keeps the process from being stopped (none was seen in 60-minute gaps) but does not make a request quick (7.4 s once) [P]; while screens are open their 8-second poll keeps it busy; and if the overnight result shows a stop, a second Custom cron call shortly before the shop opens is added (O48). On `SIGTERM` the API stops accepting calls, lets running transactions finish (at most 10 seconds) and closes the pool; a transaction still open when the process dies is rolled back by MariaDB, which releases its locks at once [S]. The app listens on `process.env.PORT` when it is set and otherwise on 3000, on `0.0.0.0` (the proof app saw no `PORT`, and Hostinger's wrapper attached it to a socket anyway [P]); logs go to stdout; Hostinger shows only the latest deployment's logs [N], so anything that must be kept goes to `sys_error_log`, `audit_log`, `check_runs` or `backup_runs`. A deploy or a change of an environment variable redeploys the app [N], so both are done when the shop is closed. **Every deploy or "Apply changes" starts the app twice, about 10 seconds apart** [P]: whatever is in memory is lost at the second start, two processes overlap while one replaces the other (so the pool of 8 per process stays under the 20 new connections a second, S11, and the tick lock keeps two ticks from running together, I8), and nobody tests for 30 seconds afterwards. **Nothing durable is written in the build folder**, which is replaced at every deploy; the backup folder and the temporary files live in the site's home folder or in `/tmp` [P]. Hostinger publishes no uptime promise and no memory-kill rule [N]: the design makes a stop or a kill harmless to the data, not invisible to the user (O44). *Test:* T-C18, T-E03, T-R01. *Source:* NODE-DESIGN 5.7, 7.2, 7.5; HOSTINGER-FACTS 2; `HOSTINGER-SPIKE.md`.

### 6.3 Import and opening data (the data side of task P3-T4)

- **IM1.** The import is a script run by Harsh on his Mac over a **connection to the production database** (never in the browser; the connection string is never in the repository). How that connection is made, Remote MySQL with his current IP allowed for the duration of the import and removed afterwards (the database password is then on his Mac and the removal is a step of the go or no-go checklist, S1, B5), or a protected command on the server, is O49. It opens at most 10 new connections in any 1.2 seconds (S11) and runs one transaction per batch (so a failed batch leaves nothing behind, and no delete is needed), each under 20 seconds and each ending as C4 says (a re-stamp of `updated_at`, then `data_epoch`), and may insert **explicit numbers**: her own `design_no`, `sku_no` and `barcode` strings are stored as given (trimmed, unique ignoring case, at most 64 characters). The billing screen finds a piece by `sku_no` or `barcode`, ignoring case, as coded (`findSkuByCode`, `designStore.ts:152-161`). *Test:* T-C16, T-E07. *Source:* PLAN P3-T4, F4, RF-2.
- **IM2.** Every batch has a row in `sys_import_batches` (the name is unique, so re-running the same batch is refused) and every imported row of `designs`, `skus`, `customers` and `bills` carries its `import_batch_id` (D21); the lines, receipts, allocations and owner-only weights of an imported bill or piece are found through their parent. The import's audit rows are written by the mechanism of L1, with no actor and the role `import`. *Test:* T-C16.
- **IM3.** After an import, the command `seedCounters()` sets each counter series to the largest number found among the existing numbers of that series (design numbers by category prefix, `sku:<design_no>` by the piece suffix, `bill:` and `opening`), and records that number as the series' `seed_value` (I5), so new numbers continue after the imported ones; L3 also skips any number that already exists. *Test:* T-C16.
- **IM4.** Rules that must not trip on imported data: I5 counts from the series' `seed_value` (IM3); I6 (re-pricing) applies only to bills that have lines; I10 holds after the import because the import calls the same `rebalance(customer)` code as X12 for every customer it gives opening rows (the import is not an API call and does not pass X12's role check); sales figures and daily cash totals exclude `opening_balance` bills and `is_opening` receipts (D15, C14). *Test:* T-B21, T-C16. *Source:* RM-7.
- **IM5.** Dues, fine and advances brought forward are loaded as opening rows (D15). A wrong figure is corrected by an owner cancelling the opening bill (`cancel_bill` accepts it) and adding a new one with X12; a wrong opening advance is voided with X4. *Test:* T-B21. *Source:* F4.
- **IM6.** **Reconciliation.** The import prints counts by table (rows in the source, rows imported, rows rejected with the reason), the total opening dues and fine against the source sheet, and each customer's `outstanding` from V2 against the source. Mobile numbers are normalised by the import (spaces, dashes and a leading +91 or 0 removed) and a blank or invalid one is listed for Siddhi. Siddhi signs the opening balances. *Test:* T-C16, T-R02.
- **IM7.** Old browser data: if the shop used the prototype, its stock and customers are read from the old browser profile **once** (an export, not a live read) and mapped; only customers' dues (as opening balances) and stock are carried over, not old estimates, unless Siddhi asks; old payments of mode `Credit` are not carried over (O17). *Test:* none: a one-off script, checked by the reconciliation (IM6, T-C16). *Source:* F4.
- **IM8.** The import runs first against the staging database (H5); it runs against production only after the reconciliation matches. *Test:* none: a rule about order, ticked in the go or no-go checklist. *Source:* reviewer risk 3.
